\iota^{\vee}:E_{j^{\prime}}\rightarrow E_{j}
then shows that there is at least one directed edge from
j^{\prime}
to
j
. When
j,j^{\prime}\notin\{0,1728\}
, taking the dual isogeny gives a bijection from the set of directed edges
j\rightarrow j^{\prime}
to the set of directed edges
j^{\prime}\rightarrow j
, so one may safely neglect orientations of such edges. This need not be the case if
j,j^{\prime}\in\{0,1728\}
.

Let
\iota:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
be an
\ell
-isogeny of
K
-CM elliptic curves. We put
\mathcal{O}\coloneqq\mathrm{End}(E)
and
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathrm{End}(E^{\prime})
.
Thus
\mathcal{O}
and
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
are each orders in
K
. Let
\mathfrak{f}
(resp.
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
) be the conductor of
\mathcal{O}
(resp. of
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
). Then by e.g.

[
BC20b
, §5.5]

we have

(7)

\frac{\mathfrak{f}}{\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}}\in\{1,\ell,\ell^{-1}\}.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be an order in an imaginary quadratic field
K
of conductor divisible by a prime
\ell
. Then no
\mathcal{O}
-CM elliptic
curve
E_{/\mathbb{C}}
admits a proper
\ell
-isogeny. Equivalently, there is no proper
\mathcal{O}
-ideal
of norm
\ell
.

Proof.

First proof:
Let
\mathfrak{p}
be an ideal of norm
\ell
, i.e.,
\#\mathcal{O}/\mathfrak{p}=\ell
. Then
\mathfrak{p}
is maximal and contains
\ell
, so

\langle\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{f}\rangle\subset\langle\mathfrak{p},\ell\rangle=\mathfrak{p}
. By
[
Co1
, Thm. 6.1]
, this means that
\mathfrak{p}
is not invertible, and
thus by
[
Co1
, Thm. 3.4]
we have that
\mathfrak{p}
is not proper.

Second proof:
Following
[
Co1
, Example 3.15]
we will show there is a unique ideal of
\mathcal{O}
of norm
\ell
and explicitly construct it. Then we will find an explicit element of
(\mathfrak{p}:\mathfrak{p})\setminus\mathcal{O}
and thereby show that
\mathfrak{p}
is not proper.
Write

\mathcal{O}_{K}=\mathbb{Z}\cdot 1\oplus\mathbb{Z}\cdot\tau_{K}.

Put
\tau\coloneqq\mathfrak{f}\tau_{K}
, so

\mathcal{O}=\mathbb{Z}\cdot 1\oplus\mathbb{Z}\cdot\tau.

Let

\mathfrak{p}\coloneqq\ell\mathbb{Z}+\mathfrak{f}\mathcal{O}_{K}.

Then as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module we have

\mathfrak{p}=\langle\ell,\mathfrak{f},\tau\rangle_{\mathbb{Z}}=\mathbb{Z}\cdot\ell\oplus\mathbb{Z}\cdot\tau,

so
[\mathcal{O}:\mathfrak{p}]=\ell
. Since
\ell\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathfrak{p}
and
\tau\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathfrak{p}
,
\mathfrak{p}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{O}
. Since it has norm
\ell
,
it is maximal. We claim that it is the unique ideal of norm
\ell
: if
\mathfrak{q}
is an ideal of
\mathcal{O}
of norm
\ell
, then
\ell=1\cdot[\mathcal{O}:\mathfrak{q}]\in\mathfrak{q}
. Since
\ell\mid\mathfrak{f}
we have

\mathfrak{p}^{2}=(\ell\mathbb{Z}+\mathfrak{f}\mathcal{O}_{K})^{2}=\ell^{2}\mathbb{Z}+\ell\mathfrak{f}\mathcal{O}_{K}+\mathfrak{f}^{2}\mathcal{O}_{K}=\ell^{2}\mathbb{Z}+\ell\mathfrak{f}\mathcal{O}_{K}\subset\ell\mathcal{O}\subset\mathfrak{q}.

Since
\mathfrak{p}
is prime this gives
\mathfrak{p}\subset\mathfrak{q}
; we have an inclusion of nonzero prime ideals in a one-dimensional domain, so

\mathfrak{p}=\mathfrak{q}
.

Now we claim that
\frac{\tau}{\ell}\in(\mathfrak{p}:\mathfrak{p})
, hence
(\mathfrak{p}:\mathfrak{p})\supsetneq\mathcal{O}
and
\mathfrak{p}
is not proper.
Let
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
\tau_{K}^{2}=a+b\tau_{K}
. Then we have

\frac{\tau}{\ell}\ell=\tau\in\mathfrak{p},\ \frac{\tau}{\ell}\tau=\frac{\mathfrak{f}^{2}}{\ell}\tau_{K}^{2}=\frac{\mathfrak{f}^{2}}{\ell}(a+b\tau_{K})=\frac{a\mathfrak{f}^{2}}{\ell}+\frac{b\mathfrak{f}}{\ell}\tau\in\mathbb{Z}\ell+\mathbb{Z}\tau=\mathfrak{p}.\qed

It follows from (
7
) that if
\varphi:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
is a cyclic
\ell
-power isogeny of
K
-CM elliptic curves,
\mathfrak{f}
is
the conductor of
\mathrm{End}(E)
and
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
is the conductor of
\mathrm{End}(E^{\prime})
, then
\frac{\mathfrak{f}}{\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}}\in\ell^{\mathbb{Z}}
. Thus the prime to
\ell
part
of the conductor is constant on each connected component of the graph
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
, so we may as well fix
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
, a
positive integer prime to
\ell
and let
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
be the induced subgraph consisting of all vertices with
prime to
\ell
conductor
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
.

The
level

L
of a vertex
j\in\mathcal{G}_{\ell,K}
is
\operatorname{ord}_{\ell}(\mathfrak{f})
, where
\mathfrak{f}
is the conductor of
\mathrm{End}(E_{j})
.
The
surface
of
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
is the induced subgraph consisting of all vertices at level
0
. Lemma
4.1
tells us that horizontal edges can only occur at the surface of
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
.

4.2.
Volcanoes

Fix a prime number
\ell
. An
\ell
-
volcano

V
is a directed graph – possibly with loops and/or multiple edges – with a partitioning of its vertex set into levels
\{V_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and a partitioning of its edge set into three
subsets, called
horizontal
,
ascending
and
descending
. Here
I
is a nonempty downward closed subset of the natural numbers
\mathbb{N}
, and thus it is either
\mathbb{N}
or
\{0,1,\ldots,d\}
for some
d\in\mathbb{N}
. The
depth
of
V
is the largest
d
such that
V_{d}
is nonempty if such a
d
exists and
\infty
otherwise. If
V
has finite
depth
d
, we we call the level
V_{d}
the
floor
. We require the following additional properties:

(0) For every edge
e:v\mapsto w

there is a canonical inverse edge
\overline{e}:w\mapsto v
.
6
6

6

Thus by identifying
e
and
\overline{e}
we get an undirected graph, from which
V
can be recovered, so this is an equivalent perspective. When we speak of the degree of a vertex, we mean
of the underlying undirected graph, and we take the convention that an edge from
x
to
x
contributes one to the degree.

(i) The subgraph
V_{0}
, called the
surface
is a regular graph of degree
s(V)\in\{0,1,2\}
. An edge is horizontal if and only if it
connects two vertices in
V_{0}
.

(ii) The ascending edges are precisely as follows: for all positive
i\in I
and each vertex
x
in
V_{i}
there is a unique
y\in V_{i-1}

and an ascending edge
e:x\mapsto v
. The descending edges are precisely the inverses of the ascending edges.

(iii) Every vertex in the floor (if any) has degree
1
.
7
7

7

This follows from the above
properties, but is worth stating explicitly.
Every other vertex
x
has degree
\ell+1
. Thus if
x
has level
i\geq 1
then it is connected to one vertex in level
i-1
and
\ell
distinct vertices in level
i+1
. If
x
has level
0
then it has
s(V)
horizontal undirected edges and
\ell+1-s(V)
descending edges emanating from
x
to distinct vertices in
V_{1}
.

For a fixed surface vertex
v_{0}
, the induced subgraph on the set of vertices that can by repeatedly descending
from
v_{0}
has the structure of a rooted tree with
v_{0}
as the root.

For the rest of this paper we will only consider the isogeny graph
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
when
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}<-4
, and we claim that in these cases
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
is an isogeny volcano of infinite depth for which each vertex at the surface has degree
1+\left(\frac{\Delta_{K}}{\ell}\right)
.
8
8

8

The converse is also true: if
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}\in\{-3,-4\}
then
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
is
not
an
\ell
-volcano. The structure of the graph is still known, and it will be recalled in
[
CS22b
]
.
In this regard, we have already shown (0).
As for (i), the surface edges emanating outward from a surface vertex correspond to proper
\mathcal{O}
-ideals of norm
\ell
. Since
\ell\nmid\mathfrak{f}_{0}
,
every
\mathcal{O}
-ideal of norm
\ell
is proper
[
Co1
, Thm. 3.1]
and the pushforward map
\mathfrak{a}\mapsto\mathfrak{a}\mathbb{Z}_{K}
is a norm-preserving
bijection from
\mathcal{O}
-ideals of norm
\ell
to
\mathbb{Z}_{K}
-ideals of norm
\ell

[
Co1
, Thm. 3.8]
. Thus we reduce to the
\mathfrak{f}_{0}=1

case, in which we certainly have
2
,
1
or
0
ideals of norm
\ell
according to whether
\ell
is split, ramified or inert in
K
. Let us look more carefully at the three cases:

Inert Case:
If
\left(\frac{\Delta_{K}}{\ell}\right)=-1
, then in the order
\mathcal{O}
of conductor
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
we have no ideals
of order
\ell
hence no surface vertices.

Ramified Case:
If
\left(\frac{\Delta_{K}}{\ell}\right)=0
, then in the order
\mathcal{O}
of conductor
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
we have a
unique prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
that is proper and of norm
\ell
. If
\mathfrak{p}
is principal then every surface vertex has a unique, self-inverse
loop. Otherwise, since
\mathfrak{p}^{2}=(\ell)
we have that
[\mathfrak{p}]
has order
2
in
\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{O}
, and the set of surface vertices is partitioned into pairs
v_{1},v_{2}
, such that if
v_{1}
corresponds to
E
then
v_{2}
corresponds to
E/E[\mathfrak{p}]
, and each of these surface edges is self-inverse. Because the ideal
\mathfrak{p}
is real, the field of moduli of
E\rightarrow E/E[\mathfrak{p}]
is
\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f}_{0})
.

Split Case:
If
\left(\frac{\Delta_{K}}{\ell}\right)=1
, then in the order
\mathcal{O}
of conductor
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
we have
(\ell)=\mathfrak{p}\overline{\mathfrak{p}}

for distinct prime ideals
\mathfrak{p}
,
\overline{\mathfrak{p}}
of norm
\ell
. Let
r
be the order of
[\mathfrak{p}]
in
\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{O}
. If
r=1
then every
surface vertex has two distinct, mutually inverse loops corresponding to
\mathfrak{p}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{p}}
. If
r=2
– equivalently, if

[\mathfrak{p}]=[\overline{\mathfrak{p}}]
then set of surface vertices is partitioned into pairs
v_{1},v_{2}
such that if
v_{1}
corresponds to
E

then
v_{2}
corresponds to
E/E[\mathfrak{p}]\cong E/E[\overline{\mathfrak{p}}]\cong E^{\prime}
, say, and there are two edges
\mathfrak{p}
,
\overline{\mathfrak{p}}
running from
v_{1}
to
v_{2}
. The inverses of these edges correspond to the isogenies
E^{\prime}\rightarrow E^{\prime}/[\overline{\mathfrak{p}}]
and
E^{\prime}\rightarrow E^{\prime}/E^{\prime}[\mathfrak{p}]
respectively. If
r\geq 3
then the set of surface vertices is naturally partitioned into
r
-cycles. Finally, as a special case of
the results on proper isogenies of the previous section, because the ideal
\mathfrak{p}
is not real, the field of moduli of
E\rightarrow E/E[\mathfrak{p}]
is
K(\mathfrak{f}_{0})
.

Now let
L\geq 1
, and let
v\in V(\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}})
be a vertex at level
L
, let
\mathfrak{f}=\ell^{L}\mathfrak{f}_{0}
,
\Delta=\mathfrak{f}^{2}\Delta_{K}
, and let
E
be the corresponding
\Delta
-CM elliptic curve. From §3.3 we get that the unique (up to equivalence)
\ell
-isogeny
from
E
to an elliptic curve with conductor
\frac{\mathfrak{f}}{\ell}
is
\iota_{\mathfrak{f},\frac{\mathfrak{f}}{\ell}}
, which has field of moduli
\mathbb{Q}(j(E))
. This shows in particular the exsistence and uniqueness of ascending edges emanating from a non-surface vertex, and clearly the descending
edges are the inverses of the ascending edges. This shows (ii).

In our setup we have no floor, and we saw in §4.1 that every vertex in
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
has outward degree
\ell+1
.
The rest of (iii) amounts to the claim that the only multiple edges and loops in
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
lie in the surface. That
there are no loops below the surface follows from Lemma
4.1
. Consider first a surface vertex
v_{0}
.
By Corollary
2.1
c), the number of vertices at level
1
is equal to
\ell-\left(\frac{\Delta_{K}}{\ell}\right)
times the
number of vertices on the surface, which is also equal to the total number of edges descending from the surface. So if two distinct downard edges emanating from
v_{0}
had the same terminal vertex
v_{1}
, then some other vertex on level
1
would not be connected to any surface vertex, contrary to what we already know. Now let
L\geq 1
and consider a vertex
v_{L}
at level
L
. Then
Corollary
2.1
c) shows that the number of vertices at level
L+1
is
\ell
times the number of vertices at level
\ell
, which is
also equal to the total number of edges descending from level
L
, so again no two edges emanating from
v_{L}
can have the same
terminal vertex. This completes our proof that when
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}<-4
the isogeny graph
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
is an
\ell
-volcano.

4.3.
Paths and
\ell^{a}
-isogenies

A
path
in a directed graph consists of a finite sequence of directed edges
e_{1},\ldots,e_{N}
such that for all
1\leq i\leq N-1
the terminal vertex of
e_{i}
is the initial vertex of
e_{i+1}
. In a directed graph in which each edge has a canonical inverse edge, a path has
backtracking
if for some
i
we have that
e_{i+1}
is the inverse edge of
e_{i}
. This is the case for
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}

in the case
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}<-4
that we consider in this paper.

The notion of a backtracking path becomes a bit more subtle in the presence of loops or multiple edges. In the
ramified case, for a path that includes surface edges
v_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{1}}}{{\rightarrow}}w_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e_{2}}}{{\rightarrow}}v_{0}
, we have

e_{2}=e_{1}^{-1}
and thus have backtracking (whether
v_{0}=w_{0}
or not). In the split case, a backtracking involving surface edges comes from traversing an edge corresponding to a prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
followed by an edge corresponding to its conjugate ideal
\overline{\mathfrak{p}}
. Thus in the split case, for a nonbacktracking path ending with a surface edge, exactly one of the two edges can be appended to retain a nonbacktracking path.

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
\Delta=\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}<-4
be an imaginary quadratic discriminant, and let
(E_{0})_{/\mathbb{C}}
be a
\Delta
-CM elliptic curve.
There is a bijective correspondence from the set of cyclic
\ell^{a}
isogenies
\varphi:E_{0}\rightarrow E_{a}
modulo isomorphism on the target
and length
a
nonbacktracking paths in the isogeny graph
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
with initial vertex
j(E_{0})
.

Proof.

Let
\varphi:E_{0}\rightarrow E_{a}
be a cyclic
\ell^{a}
-isogeny of elliptic curves, each with
K
-CM and prime-to-
\ell
-conductor
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
. As in §3.2, the isogeny
\varphi
factors uniquely into a length
a
sequence of
\ell
-isogenies
\varphi_{i}:E_{i}\rightarrow E_{i+1}
, and in this way we get a path of length
a
in
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
. A backtracking in
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
corresponds to performing an
\ell
-isogeny
\iota
followed by its
dual isogeny
\iota^{\vee}
and thus
\varphi
would factor through
[\ell]
and fail to be cyclic.

Conversely, a path of length
a
in
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
yields a sequence of cyclic
\ell
-isogenies
\{\varphi_{i}:E_{i}\rightarrow E_{i+1}\}_{i=0}^{a-1}
and then
\varphi\coloneqq\varphi_{a-1}\circ\ldots\varphi_{0}
is an
\ell^{a}
-isogeny. It remains to see
that the lack of backtracking implies that
\varphi
is cyclic. This comes down to: for
1\leq A\leq a
, if
\varphi=\varphi_{A-1}\circ\ldots\circ\varphi_{0}:E_{0}\rightarrow E_{A}
is a cyclic
k
-isogeny and
\psi:E_{A}\rightarrow E^{\prime}
is an
\ell
-isogeny, then if

\operatorname{Ker}\psi\neq\operatorname{Ker}\varphi_{A-1}^{\vee}
then
\psi\circ\varphi
is a cyclic
\ell^{A+1}
-isogeny. Via uniformizing lattices this
translates to the following claim: let

\Lambda_{0}\subset\Lambda_{1}\subset\ldots\subset\Lambda_{A}\subset\mathbb{C}

be lattices with
[\Lambda_{i+1}:\Lambda_{i}]=\ell
for all
i
and
\Lambda_{A}/\Lambda_{0}
cyclic of order
\ell^{A}
. Then of the
\ell+1
lattices
\Lambda^{\prime}\subset\mathbb{C}
that contain
\Lambda
with index
\ell
, there is exactly one such that

\Lambda^{\prime}/\Lambda_{0}
is not cyclic, namely
\frac{1}{\ell}\Lambda_{A-1}
. Indeed, by the structure theory of finitely generated

\mathbb{Z}
-modules there is a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
e_{1},e_{2}
for
\Lambda_{A}
such that
e_{1},\ell^{A}e_{2}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis for
\Lambda_{0}
and
thus
e_{1},\ell e_{2}
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis for
\Lambda_{A-1}
. The
\ell
-torsion subgroup of
(\Lambda_{0}\otimes\mathbb{Q})/\Lambda_{0}
is
generated by
\frac{1}{\ell}e_{1}
and
\ell^{A-1}e_{2}
, so a subgroup of
(\Lambda_{0}\otimes\mathbb{Q})/\Lambda_{0}
that contains

\Lambda_{1}/\Lambda_{0}
has full
\ell
-torsion if and only if it contains
\frac{1}{\ell}e_{1}
. But
\frac{1}{\ell}\Lambda_{A-1}
is the unique
lattice containing
\Lambda_{A}
with index
\ell
and containing
\frac{1}{\ell}e_{1}
.
∎

Lemma
4.2
allows us to enumerate and then study cyclic prime power isogenies of CM elliptic curves in terms of nonbacktracking
finite paths in
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
. The key to this enumeration is that nonbacktracking paths in
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
have a restricted form: every such path
P
uniquely decomposes as a concatenation
P_{3}\circ P_{2}\circ P_{1}
of three paths, though some of them may have length
0
. Namely,
P_{1}
consists entirely of ascending edges,
P_{2}
consists entirely of horizontal edges and
P_{3}

consists entirely of descending edges. To see this we observe that an equivalent statement is that a surface edge can only be followed by another surface edge or
descending edge, while a descending edge can only be followed by a descending edge. The former statement is clear – as we are
at the surface, we cannot ascend – and once we have descended, we are not at the surface so have no horizontal edges and a descent
followed by an ascent is a backtrack.

4.4.
Reality, part II: coreality

In this section we will determine the isomorphism class of the number field generated by the
j
-invariants of two elliptic curves
with CM by the same imaginary quadratic field
K
, assuming that
\Delta_{K}<-4
.

Let
j,j^{\prime}\in\mathbb{C}
be
K
-CM
j
-invariants, of discriminants
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}
and conductors
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
.

We say
j,j^{\prime}
are
coreal
if the number field
\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime})
admits a real embedding.

Suppose first that there is a prime number
\ell
and integers
a\geq a^{\prime}\geq 1
such that
\mathfrak{f}=\ell^{a}
,
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}=\ell^{a^{\prime}}
. In this case
we claim that
j,j^{\prime}
are coreal if for any field embedding
\iota:\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
, we have
\iota(j)\in\mathbb{R}\implies\iota(j^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}
.
The latter condition is clearly sufficient for coreality: indeed, there is an embedding
\iota:\mathbb{Q}(j)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{R}\subset\mathbb{C}
, so
our assumption gives

\iota(\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime}))=\mathbb{Q}(\iota(j),\iota(j^{\prime}))\subset\mathbb{R}.

Conversely, suppose that
\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime})
admits a
real embedding, and let
\iota:\mathbb{Q}(j,j)\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
be such that
\iota(j)\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
\iota(j)\in K(j_{\Delta})^{c}=\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})
. Since
a^{\prime}\leq a
we have
\mathbb{Q}(\iota(j^{\prime}))\subset K(\mathfrak{f})
and
\mathbb{Q}(\iota(j),\iota(j^{\prime}))=K(\mathfrak{f})
if and only if
\iota(j^{\prime})\notin\mathbb{R}
.
Since
j,j^{\prime}
are coreal we have
\mathbb{Q}(\iota(j),\iota(j^{\prime}))\cong\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime})
admits a real embedding, so it cannot contain
K
and thus
\iota(j^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}
.

In the above setup, to determine the coreality of
j,j^{\prime}
we may reduce via simultaneous Galois conjugacy to the case
j=j_{\Delta}
, and then the set of

\Delta^{\prime}
-CM
j
-invariants
j^{\prime}
such that
j,j^{\prime}
are coreal are precisely the real roots of the Hilbert class polynomial
H_{\Delta^{\prime}}(t)
,
of which there are precisely
h_{2}(\Delta^{\prime})\coloneqq\#\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{O}(\Delta^{\prime})[2]
. If
j,j^{\prime}
are coreal we have
\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime})\cong\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})
,
while if
j,j^{\prime}
are not coreal, we have
\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime})=K(\mathfrak{f})
.

Everything done above goes through verbatim in the somewhat more general case that
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}\mid\mathfrak{f}
: namely
j,j^{\prime}
are coreal
if and only if for all
\iota:\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{C}
we have
\iota(j)\in\mathbb{R}\implies\iota(j^{\prime})\in\mathbb{R}
, so we may Galois conjguate to the
case
j=j_{\Delta}
, and then
\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime})\cong\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})
if
j,j^{\prime}
are coreal and
\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime})=K(\mathfrak{f})
otherwise. This includes the case
in which
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}=1
and in particular the case in which
\mathfrak{f}=\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}=1
.

We remark that if
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}\nmid\mathfrak{f}
it can happen that
j,j^{\prime}
are coreal,
j\in\mathbb{R}
and
j^{\prime}\notin\mathbb{R}
. For instance, suppose that

K=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-7})
and
\mathfrak{f}=1
, so
j\in\mathbb{Q}
. Then
\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime})=\mathbb{Q}(j^{\prime})\cong\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})
has a real embedding, so
j,j^{\prime}
are coreal,
but for all sufficiently large
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}
there are non-real
(\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})^{2}\Delta_{K}
-CM
j
-invariants.

We now return to the general case: we have

j=j(E)
,
j^{\prime}=j(E^{\prime})
, where
E
(resp.
E^{\prime}
) is a
\Delta
-CM elliptic curve (resp.
\Delta^{\prime}
-CM elliptic curve) for
\Delta=\mathfrak{f}^{2}\Delta_{K}
(resp.
\Delta^{\prime}=(\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})^{2}\Delta_{K}
). We put
M\coloneqq\operatorname{lcm}(\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})
.

There is a canonical
\mathbb{Q}(j)
-rational isogeny from
E
to a
\Delta_{K}
-CM elliptic curve

E_{0}
and we put
j_{0}\coloneqq j(E_{0})
; similarly we define
j_{0}^{\prime}=j(E_{0}^{\prime})
. If
j_{0},j_{0}^{\prime}
are coreal then
\mathbb{Q}(j_{0},j_{0}^{\prime})=\mathbb{Q}(1)
,
while if
j_{0},j_{0}^{\prime}
are not coreal then
\mathbb{Q}(j_{0},j_{0}^{\prime})=K(1)
, the Hilbert class field of
K
. Since
\mathbb{Q}(j_{0},j_{0}^{\prime})\subset\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime})
,
if
j_{0},j_{0}^{\prime}
are not coreal then
\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime})
contains
K
and then it is easy to see that
\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime})=K(M)
.

From now on we assume that
j_{0},j_{0}^{\prime}
are coreal and thus
\mathbb{Q}(j_{0})=\mathbb{Q}(j_{0}^{\prime})=F_{0}
, say. We treat this case by a primary decomposition argument. Write

\mathfrak{f}=\ell_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots\ell_{r}^{a_{r}},\ \mathfrak{f}^{\prime}=\ell_{1}^{a_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots\ell_{r}^{a_{r}^{\prime}},a_{i},a_{i}\in\mathbb{N},A_{i}\coloneqq\max(a_{i},a_{i}^{\prime})\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}.

For all
1\leq i\leq r
, there is a canonical
\mathbb{Q}(j)
-rational isogeny from
E
to a
(\Delta_{i}=\ell_{i}^{2a_{i}}\Delta_{K})
-CM elliptic curve

E_{i}
, and we put
j_{i}=j(E_{i})
, and in a similar way we define
E_{i}^{\prime}
and
j_{i}^{\prime}=j(E_{i}^{\prime})
. If we Galois
conjugate
j
to
j_{\Delta}
then each
j_{i}
gets Galois conjugated to
j_{\Delta_{i}}
, so it follows from Proposition
2.10
that
\mathbb{Q}(j)=\mathbb{Q}(j_{1},\ldots,j_{r})
, and similarly we have
\mathbb{Q}(j^{\prime})=\mathbb{Q}(j_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,j_{r}^{\prime})
. Put

M=\operatorname{lcm}(\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})
. If for some
1\leq i\leq r
we have that
j_{i},j_{i}^{\prime}
are
not
coreal, then

\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime})=K(M)=K(\ell_{1}^{A_{1}}\cdots\ell_{r}^{A_{r}}).

Otherwise we have that
j_{i},j_{i}^{\prime}
are coreal for all
1\leq i\leq r
, so for each
1\leq i\leq r
, we have
F_{i}\coloneqq\mathbb{Q}(j_{i},j_{i}^{\prime})\supset\mathbb{Q}(j_{0})
. It follows from Proposition
2.10
and an easy inductive argument that

\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime})=F_{1}\cdots F_{r}\cong F_{1}\otimes_{F_{0}}F_{2}\otimes_{F_{0}}\cdots\otimes_{F_{0}}F_{r}\cong\mathbb{Q}(M).

In particular, we get:

Theorem 4.3
.

Let
K
be an imaginary quadratic field with
\Delta_{K}<-4
, and let
j,j^{\prime}
be
K
-CM
j
-invariants, of conductors
\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{f}^{\prime\prime}
, and put
M\coloneqq\operatorname{lcm}(\mathfrak{f},\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})
.

a)

If
j,j^{\prime}
are coreal, then
\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime})\cong\mathbb{Q}(M)
.

b)

If
j,j^{\prime}
are not coreal, then
\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime})=K(M)
.

Proof.

We saw above that
\mathbb{Q}(j,j^{\prime})
is either isomorphic to
\mathbb{Q}(M)
or equal to
K(M)
. Since
\mathbb{Q}(M)
has a real embedding and

K(M)
does not, the result follows.
∎

The following is an immediate consequence.

Corollary 4.4
.

Let
K
be an imaginary quadratic field with
\Delta_{K}<-4
.

a)

The compositum of finitely many ring class fields of
K
is a ring class field of
K
.

b)

The compositum of finitely many rational ring class fields of
K
is either a rational ring class field of
K
or a ring class
field of
K
.

5.
Action of Complex Conjugation on
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}

Fix
K
and
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
. Let
\ell
be a prime number. In this section and the next we will define and then explicitly determine an
action of complex conjugation – that is, of the group
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}=\{1,c\}
– on
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
by graph automorphisms.
9
9

9

There are in fact two cases —
(\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta,\ell)\in\{(-4,2),(-3,3)\}
— in which we
cannot
define this action on the graph
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
but only on a certain double cover.

This action is one of the key points of the entire work: it gives us the leverage we need to analyze isogenies of CM elliptic curves over

\mathbb{Q}(j)
and not just over
K(j)
.

We begin with the observation that in all cases there is a natural transitive action of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{C})
on the vertex set of
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
: it is indeed just the action of
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{C})
on
\mathbb{C}
restricted to the subset of elliptic curves with
\ell^{2L}\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}
-CM. This action factors through an action of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{Q}}=\operatorname{Aut}(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
. We pause to observe that this action preserves the level and for all
L\in\mathbb{Z}^{\geq 0}
acts transitively on the set of vertices at level
L
.

If vertices
v,w\in\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
correspond to elliptic curves
E_{v}
and
E_{w}
over
\mathbb{C}
, then there is an edge from
v
to
w
if and only if there is a cyclic order

\ell
-subgroup
C
of
E_{v}
such that
E_{v}/C\cong E_{w}
. Then for any
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{C})
we have
\sigma(E_{v})/\sigma(C)\cong\sigma(E_{w})
, showing there is an edge from
\sigma(v)
to
\sigma(w)
. More precsiely
\sigma
gives a bijection from the subset
of order
\ell
subgroups
C
of
E_{v}
such that
E_{v}/C\cong E_{w}
to the subset of order
\ell
subgroups
C
of
E_{\sigma(v)}

such that
E_{\sigma(v)}/C\cong E_{\sigma(w)}
.

What we have done so far defines an action of
\operatorname{Aut}\mathbb{C}
on
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
precisely in the absence of multiple directed
edges running between the same pair of vertices. In these cases we need to say more, because the isogeny graph takes into account
only the
number
of edges from
v
to
w
: we have a bijection from the set of edges from
v
to
w
to the set of
order
\ell
subgroups
C
of
E_{v}
such that
E_{v}/C\cong E_{w}
but not (yet) a
canonical
bijection.

Under our assumption that
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}<-4
, the only possible multiple edges are multiple surface edges. Such edges exist precisely when we are in the split case —
\left(\frac{\Delta_{K}}{\ell}\right)=1
— and the two prime ideals

\mathfrak{p}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{p}}
of the order of
\mathcal{O}
of conductor
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
lying over
\ell
are principal, in which case we have two surface loops at each surface vertex
v
. In this case, for
\sigma\in\operatorname{Aut}(\mathbb{C})
we decree that
\sigma
fixes each of the two surface loops if and only if it acts trivially on
K
.

To justify our definition, let
F
be a field with
\mathbb{Q}(j(E_{v}))\subseteq F\subseteq\mathbb{C}
and let
E_{/F}
be
any

elliptic curve with
j
-invariant
j(E_{v})
.
The two surface loops are realized by the subgroup schemes
E[\mathfrak{p}]
and
E[\overline{\mathfrak{p}}]
(in some order!), and for any
ideal
I
of
\mathcal{O}
and
\sigma\in\mathfrak{g}_{F}
, we have

\sigma:E[I]\mapsto E[\sigma(I)].

Remark 5.1
.

Let
v\in\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
, and let
j_{v}
be the corresponding point on
X(1)(\overline{\mathbb{Q}})
. Since
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}<4
, the set of edges
e
with initial vertex
v
is in
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{Q}}
-equivariant bijection with the fiber of
X_{0}(\ell)\rightarrow X(1)
over the geometric
point
j_{v}
.

Now let
c
be the image of complex conjugation in
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{Q}}
. We call a vertex or an edge of
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}

real
if it is fixed by complex conjuation and
complex
otherwise. Complex vertices and edges occur in conjugate pairs.

We begin with some simple but useful observations concerning this definition:

\bullet
Complex conjugation maps ascending edges to ascending edges, horizontal edges to horizontal edges, and descending
edges to descending edges.

\bullet
An edge
e\in\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
determines a point
P_{e}\in X_{0}(\ell)(\mathbb{C})
. Like any curve defined over
\mathbb{Q}
,
X_{0}(\ell)
has a canonical
\mathbb{R}
-model, which determines an action of complex conjugation on
X_{0}(\ell)(\mathbb{C})
. Under this action we have

c(P_{e})=P_{c(e)}
, a special case of Remark
5.1
. In particular,
e
is real if and only if
P_{e}\in X_{0}(\ell)(\mathbb{R})
.

\bullet
An edge is real if and only if its inverse edge is real.

\bullet
If an edge
e:v\mapsto w
is real, then both
v
and
w
are real. The converse also holds except for surface
edges in the split case: as explained above, these edges are complex, but they exist for every surface vertex. In the split case,
let
v
be a real surface vertex, and let
E_{v}
be the corresponding complex elliptic curve. Let
\mathfrak{p}
and
\overline{\mathfrak{p}}
be the
two primes of
\mathcal{O}
lying over
\ell
. Then
c(E/E[\mathfrak{p}])=c(E)/c(E[\mathfrak{p}])=E/E[\overline{\mathfrak{p}}]
. We have

E/E[\overline{\mathfrak{p}}]\cong_{\mathbb{C}}E/[\mathfrak{p}]
if and only if
[\mathfrak{p}]=[\overline{\mathfrak{p}}]
in
\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{O}
. Since
\mathfrak{p}\overline{\mathfrak{p}}=(\ell)

this holds if and only if
\mathfrak{p}\in(\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{O})[2]
.

\bullet
For vertices
v
and
w
in
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
, there is a unique edge
e
from
v
to
w
if and only if there is a unique
edge
c(e)
from
c(v)
to
c(w)
. When this occurs, knowing
c(v)
and
c(w)
determines
c(e)
. In particular, in this case
the converse of the above observation holds:
e:v\mapsto w
is real if and only if
v
and
w
are real.

\bullet
In the ramified case, a surface edge

e:v\mapsto w
is real if and only if
v
is real if and only if
w
is real.

An ascending edge
e:v\mapsto w
is real if and only
v
is real: clearly if

e
is real, then so is
v
, and conversely, if
v
is real, then
c(e)
is an ascending edge emanating from
v
, of which
e
is the only one. By passing to inverses, we deduce that a descending edge
e:v\mapsto w
is real if and only if
w
is real.

5.1.
The field of moduli of a cyclic
\ell^{a}
-isogeny

The following result computes the
field of moduli of a cyclic
\ell^{a}
-isogeny of CM elliptic curves in the
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}<-4
case.

Theorem 5.1
.

Let
\varphi:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
be a cyclic
\ell^{a}
-isogeny of CM elliptic curves with
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}<-4
. Let
\mathfrak{f}
be the maximum
of the conductor of
\mathrm{End}(E)
and the conductor of
\mathrm{End}(E^{\prime})
.

a)

If
\ell
splits in
K
and
\varphi
factors through an
\ell
-isogeny of
\mathfrak{f}_{0}\Delta_{K}
-CM elliptic curves, then

\mathbb{Q}(\varphi)=K(\mathfrak{f})
. In every other case we have
\mathbb{Q}(\varphi)=\mathbb{Q}(j(E),j(E^{\prime}))
.

b)

If
j(E)
and
j(E^{\prime})
are not coreal then we have
\mathbb{Q}(\varphi)=K(\mathfrak{f})
.

c)

Suppose that
j(E)
and
j(E^{\prime})
are coreal. Then if the conductor of
E^{\prime}
divides the conductor of
E
we have

\mathbb{Q}(j(E),j(E^{\prime}))=\mathbb{Q}(j(E))
, while if the conductor of
E
divides the conductor of
E^{\prime}
we have
\mathbb{Q}(j(E),j(E^{\prime}))=\mathbb{Q}(j(E^{\prime}))
.

Proof.

Let
\Delta=\ell^{2L}\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}
(resp.
\Delta^{\prime}=\ell^{2L^{\prime}}\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}
) be the discriminant of the endomorphism ring
of
E
(resp. of
E^{\prime}
). We have
\mathbb{Q}(\varphi)=\mathbb{Q}(\varphi^{\vee})
, and
the assertions of Theorem
5.1
hold for
\varphi
if and only if they hold for
\varphi^{\vee}
, so by replacing
\varphi
with

\varphi^{\vee}
is necessary we may assume that
L\geq L^{\prime}
, so that the conductor
\ell^{L^{\prime}}\mathfrak{f}_{0}
of
E^{\prime}
divides the conductor

\ell^{L}\mathfrak{f}_{0}
of
E
.

For
\sigma\in\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, we have
\mathbb{Q}(\sigma(\varphi))=\sigma(\mathbb{Q}(\varphi))\cong\mathbb{Q}(\varphi)
, so up to replacing the field of moduli by an isomorphic number field we may replace
\iota
by
\sigma(\iota):\sigma(E)\rightarrow\sigma(E^{\prime})
and thus we may assume that
j(E)=j_{\Delta}
.

As in §4.3,
\varphi
determines a nonbacktracking path
P(\varphi)
of length
a
in
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
. Now put
E_{0}\coloneqq E
,
E_{a}\coloneqq E^{\prime}
; for
0\leq i\leq a
, let
\varphi_{i}:E_{i}\rightarrow E_{i+1}
be the
\ell
-isogeny corresponding to the
i
th edge of the path
P
. By Proposition
3.1
b) we have
\mathbb{Q}(\varphi)=\mathbb{Q}(\varphi_{1})\cdots\mathbb{Q}(\varphi_{a})
. Then
K(\mathfrak{f})\supseteq\mathbb{Q}(j(E),j(E^{\prime}))
. Each ascending
\ell
-isogeny
\varphi_{i}:E_{i}\rightarrow E_{i+1}
is defined over

\mathbb{Q}(j(E_{i}))\subseteq K(\mathfrak{f})
; each horizontal edge is defined over
K(\mathfrak{f}_{0})\subseteq K(\mathfrak{f})
; and each descending

\ell
-isogeny
\varphi_{i}:E_{i}\rightarrow E_{i+1}
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}(j(E_{i+1})\subseteq K(\mathfrak{f})
. So we have

\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})=\mathbb{Q}(j(E))\subseteq\mathbb{Q}(j(E),j(E^{\prime}))\subseteq\mathbb{Q}(\varphi)\subseteq K(\mathfrak{f}).

If
\ell
splits in
K
and for some
i
the
\ell
-isogeny
\varphi_{i}:E_{i}\rightarrow E_{i+1}
induces a horizontal edge, then
K\subset\mathbb{Q}(\varphi_{i})\subset\mathbb{Q}(\varphi)
, and thus we must have
\mathbb{Q}(\varphi)=K(\mathfrak{f})
. Next suppose that
P(\varphi)
contains no such edge. Then

\mathbb{Q}(\varphi)=\mathbb{Q}(j(E_{0}),\ldots,j(E_{a}))
.

Let
v
and
w
are two vertices in
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
corresponding
to elliptic curves
E_{v}
and
E_{w}
. If there is a path from
v
to
w
consisting entirely of ascending edges, then

\mathbb{Q}(j(E_{v}))\supset\mathbb{Q}(j(E_{w}))
. In the case that
\ell
ramifies in
K
, if
e:v\rightarrow w
is a horizontal edge, then
\mathbb{Q}(j(E_{v}))=\mathbb{Q}(j(E_{w}))
. From this we deduce:

\mathbb{Q}(\varphi)=\mathbb{Q}(j(E_{0}),\ldots,j(E_{a}))=\mathbb{Q}(j(E_{0}),j(E_{a}))=\mathbb{Q}(j(E),j(E^{\prime})).

This establishes part a). Part b) is immediate from Theorem
4.3
. As for part c), we have reduced to the case
L^{\prime}\leq L
,
so if
j(E)
and
j(E^{\prime})
are coreal then after our Galois conjugation we have
j(E^{\prime})\in K(\mathfrak{f})\cap\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})=\mathbb{Q}(j(E))
, so
\mathbb{Q}(j(E),j(E^{\prime}))=\mathbb{Q}(j(E))
. But this identity is unchanged by replacing
j(E)
and
j(E^{\prime})
by
\sigma(j(E))
and
\sigma(j(E^{\prime}))
for any
\sigma\in\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{Q}}
, so indeed we have
\mathbb{Q}(j(E),j(E^{\prime}))=\mathbb{Q}(j(E))
.
∎

Our next major task is to fix an imaginary quadratic discriminant
\Delta=\ell^{2L}\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}
with
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}<-4
and a prime power
\ell^{a}
and to compute the fiber of
X_{0}(\ell^{a})\rightarrow X(1)
over
J_{\Delta}
. In order to do this, as above we may consider
cylic
\ell^{a}
-isogenies
\varphi:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
such that
j(E)=j_{\Delta}
, and as we range over all length
a
nonbacktracking paths in

\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
with terminal vertex
w
corresponding to an elliptic curve
E^{\prime}
, we need to understand for which of these
paths we have that
j_{\Delta}
and
j(E^{\prime})
are coreal. For this we need a more explicit description of the action of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}

on
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
, which we provide in the next section. We also need to modify the above approach slightly, since
switching to the dual isogeny so as to ensure that
j(E)
has level at least as large as the level of
j(E^{\prime})
is not a good approach to the coming combinatorial problem. We handle the latter first:

Suppose that we have a nonbacktracking path
P
of length
a
in
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
corresponding to
\varphi:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
,
and such that
E
is
\Delta=\ell^{2L}\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}
-CM and
E^{\prime}
is
\Delta^{\prime}=\ell^{2L^{\prime}}\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}
-CM with
L^{\prime}>L
, and
put
\mathfrak{f}=\ell^{L}\mathfrak{f}_{0}
,
\mathfrak{f}^{\prime}=\ell^{L^{\prime}}\mathfrak{f}_{0}
. By the above analysis, the field of moduli
\mathbb{Q}(\varphi)
is either
\mathbb{Q}(j(E^{\prime}))
(which is isomorphic though not necessarily equal to
\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})
) or
K(j(E^{\prime}))=K(\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})
. If the path
P
contains a horizontal edge in the split case
then we have
\mathbb{Q}(\varphi)=K(\mathfrak{f}^{\prime})
, so suppose that is not the case. Then we have
\mathbb{Q}(\varphi)=\mathbb{Q}(j(E^{\prime}))
if and only if
j_{\Delta}
and

j(E^{\prime})
are coreal. Let
P_{1}
be the maximal initial segment of the path
P
that terminates at a vertex in level
L
, and let
P_{2}
be the
rest of the path, so
P_{2}
consists entirely of
L^{\prime}-L
descending edges. Let
a_{1}<a
be the length of
P_{1}
, and let

\varphi_{1}:E\rightarrow E_{1}
be the corresponding factor isogeny. Then
\mathbb{Q}(j(E_{1}))\subset\mathbb{Q}(j(E^{\prime}))
, so if
j_{\Delta}
and
j(E^{\prime})
are
coreal then so are
j_{\Delta}
and
j(E_{1})
, and since
E
and
E_{1}
have the same endomorphism ring, this occurs if and only if
j(E_{1})\in\mathbb{R}
.
Conversely, if
j(E_{1})\in\mathbb{R}
then
\mathbb{Q}(j(E_{1}))=\mathbb{Q}(j_{\Delta})

and thus

\mathbb{Q}(j_{\Delta},j(E^{\prime}))=\mathbb{Q}(j(E_{1}),j(E^{\prime}))=\mathbb{Q}(j(E^{\prime})).

Since we wish to count closed points in the fiber of
X_{0}(\ell^{a})\rightarrow X(1)
over
J_{\Delta}
, we need to impose an equivalence relation
on paths: any path in the same
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})}
-orbit as
P(\varphi)
determines the same closed point on
X_{0}(\ell^{a})
as

P(\varphi)
. The size of this Galois orbit is

d_{\varphi}\coloneqq[\mathbb{Q}(\varphi):\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})].

Complex conjugation acts on paths in
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
, and a path is real if and only if each of its edges is real.

Lemma 5.2
.

We maintain notation as above. Put

\epsilon_{\varphi}\coloneqq\begin{cases}1&P_{1}\text{ is real}\\
2&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}.

Then:

a)

If
L\geq L^{\prime}
, then we have
d_{\varphi}=\epsilon_{\varphi}
.

b)

If
L=0
and
L^{\prime}>L
, then we have
d_{\varphi}=\epsilon_{\varphi}(\ell+\left(\frac{\Delta_{K}}{\ell}\right))\ell^{L^{\prime}-L-1}
.

c)

If
0<L<L^{\prime}
, then we have
d_{\varphi}=\epsilon_{\varphi}\ell^{L^{\prime}-L}
.

Proof.

This follows easily from the description of
\mathbb{Q}(\varphi)
we have given.
∎

We can also explicitly describe the
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})}
-orbit on
P(\varphi)
: it consists of all paths obtained from
P_{1}
by descending
\max(L^{\prime}-L,0)
times as well as all paths obtained from
\overline{P_{1}}
by descending
\max(L^{\prime}-L,0)
times. We will say that
two paths in the same
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})}
-orbit are
closed point equivalent
.

5.2.
Cyclic
N
-isogenies on real elliptic curves

Theorem 5.3
.

Let
E_{/\mathbb{R}}
be an elliptic curve. For
N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, let
\mathcal{C}_{N}(E)
denote the set of cyclic order
N
subgroups
of
E(\mathbb{C})
, and let
\mathcal{C}_{N}(E)^{c}
denote the subset that is fixed under the natural action of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}=\{1,c\}
. Let
t
be the number of real roots
f
, where
y^{2}=f(x)
is a polynomial defining
E_{/\mathbb{R}}
. Suppose that
N
has
r
distinct odd prime divisors.

a)

If
N
is odd, then
\#\mathcal{C}_{N}(E)^{c}=2^{r}
.

b)

If
N\equiv 2\pmod{4}
, then
\#\mathcal{C}_{N}(E)^{c}=t\cdot 2^{r}
.

c)

If
N\equiv 4\pmod{8}
, then
\#\mathcal{C}_{N}(E)^{c}\in\{2^{r+1},2^{r+2}\}
.

Proof.

Step 1: Write
N=\ell_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots\ell_{R}^{a_{R}}
with
2\leq\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}<\ldots<\ell_{R}
. There is a
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}
-equivariant
bijection from
\prod_{i=1}^{R}\mathcal{C}_{\ell_{i}^{a_{i}}}(E)
to
\mathcal{C}_{N}(E)
obtained by mapping the tuple
(C_{1},\ldots,C_{R})
to the subgroup
\langle C_{1},\ldots,C_{R}\rangle
. This reduces us to the case of
N=\ell^{a}
a prime power.

Step 2: Suppose that
N=\ell
is an odd prime. We recall a version of a well-known fact: let
R
be a commutative ring with
2\in R^{\times}
, let
M
be an
R
-module, and let
c\in\mathrm{End}_{R}(M)
satisfy

c^{2}=1
, and put

M_{+}\coloneqq\{x\in M\mid c(x)=x\}\text{ and }M_{-}\coloneqq\{x\in M\mid c(x)=-x\}.

Then we have
M=M_{+}\oplus M_{-}
. Indeed, if
x\in M_{+}\cap M_{-}
, then
2x=0
, so
x=0
. Also, for
x\in M
we may
write
x=\frac{x+c(x)}{2}+\frac{x-c(x)}{2}
.

Step 3: Suppose that
N=\ell^{a}
is an odd prime power. We apply Step 2 with
R=\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{a}\mathbb{Z}
,
M=E[\ell^{a}](\mathbb{C})
and take
c
to
be complex conjugation, so

E[\ell^{a}](\mathbb{C})=E[\ell^{a}](\mathbb{C})_{+}\oplus E[\ell^{a}](\mathbb{C})_{-}.

We have
E[\ell^{a}](\mathbb{C})_{+}=E[\ell^{a}](\mathbb{R})
. Since
E(\mathbb{R})
is isomorphic to either
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
, we have (using that
\ell>2
) that
E[\ell^{a}](\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{a}\mathbb{Z}
; since
E[\ell^{a}](\mathbb{C})\cong(\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{a}\mathbb{Z})^{2}
, it follows that
E[\ell^{a}](\mathbb{C})_{-}\cong\mathbb{Z}/\ell^{a}\mathbb{Z}
. Thus
E[\ell^{a}](\mathbb{C})_{+}
and
E[\ell^{a}](\mathbb{C})_{-}
are two elements of
\mathcal{C}_{\ell^{a}}(E)^{c}
. If there were any others,
there would be
x\in E[\ell^{a}](\mathbb{C})
and an integer
n
prime to
\ell
such that
c(x)=nx
and, in the unique
representation of
x
as
x_{+}+x_{-}
with
x_{+}\in E[\ell^{a}](\mathbb{C})_{+}
and
x_{-}\in E[\ell^{a}](\mathbb{C})_{0}
, we have
x_{+},x_{-}\neq 0
, so each has order divisible by
\ell
. Then

x_{+}-x_{-}=c(x)=nx=nx_{+}+nx_{-},

which shows that
n\equiv 1\pmod{\ell}
and
n\equiv-1\pmod{\ell}
, a contradiction.

This completes the proof of part a). Part b) follows immediately, since
t=\#\mathcal{C}_{2}(E)
.

Step 4: Let
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. The homomorphism

[2]:E(\mathbb{C})[2^{a+1}]\rightarrow E(\mathbb{C})[2^{a}]

is
\mathfrak{g}_{R}
-equivariant, surjective and such that each fiber is a principal homogeneous space under
E(\mathbb{C})[2]
. There is an induced

\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}
-equivariant map

[2]:\mathcal{C}_{2^{a+1}}(E)\rightarrow\mathcal{C}_{2^{a}}(E)

that is surjective, with each fiber of size
2
.

Case 1: Suppose that
t=1
, so
E(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
. We claim that
\#\mathcal{C}_{4}(E)^{c}=2
. If
C\in\mathcal{C}_{4}(E)^{c}
, then also
[2]C\in\mathcal{C}_{2}(E)^{c}
, and there is a unique such order
2
subgroup,
say,
C_{2}
, so it follows that
\#\mathcal{C}_{4}(E)^{c}\leq 2
. On the other hand, since
E(\mathbb{R})[4]\cong\mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z}
we certainly have
\#\mathcal{C}_{4}(E)^{c}\geq 1
. It follows that at least one of the two subgroups
C
such that
[2]C=C_{2}
is
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}
-stable, so
the other such subgroup must be
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}
-stable as well.

Case 2: Suppose that
t=3
, so
E(\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
and thus
E(\mathbb{R})[4]\cong\mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
.
Then
\#\mathcal{C}_{4}(E)^{c}\in\{0,2,4,6\}
, depending upon how many of the
3
elements of
\mathcal{C}_{2}(E)^{c}
lift to a pair of elements
on which complex conjugation acts trivially. The
4
elements of
E(\mathbb{R})
of order
4
map to
2
elements of
\mathcal{C}_{2}(E)^{c}
,
so
\#\mathcal{C}_{4}(E)^{c}\geq 2
. For any other element
C\in\mathcal{C}_{4}(E)^{c}
, if
P
generates
C
then
\overline{P}=-P
. So if
\#\mathcal{C}_{4}(E)^{c}=6
then
E(\mathbb{C})
has eight elements of order
4
that are inverted by complex conjugation. But in the group

E(\mathbb{C})[4]\cong\mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z}
the largest number of elements of order
4
that generate a proper subgroup is
4
, so if

E(\mathbb{C})
had eight elements of order
4
inverted by complex conjugation, then complex conjugation would have to act on
E(\mathbb{C})[4]

by
-1
, which we know is not the case.
∎

Corollary 5.4
.

Let
E_{/\mathbb{R}}
be an elliptic curve, and let
N\geq 3
. Then the projective
N
-torsion field
\mathbb{R}(\mathbb{P}E[N])
of
E
is
\mathbb{C}
.

Proof.

It follows from Theorem
5.3
that
\#\mathcal{C}_{N}(E)^{c}<\#\mathcal{C}_{N}(E)
.
∎

5.3.
Explicit action of complex conjugation on
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}

Suppose
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}<-4
. We give an explicit description of the action of complex conjugation on the isogeny volcano – up to
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{R}}
-equivariant graph-theoretic isomorphism –
in all cases. For
L\geq 0
, put

(8)

\mathfrak{r}_{L}\coloneqq\#\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{O}(\ell^{2L}\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K})[2].

By Corollary
2.5
,
\mathfrak{r}_{L}
is the number of real vertices in
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
at level
L
. Lemma
2.8
computes
\mathfrak{r}_{L}
in terms of

\mathfrak{r}_{0}
.

Figure 1.
Corollary
5.5

\ell>2
split

Figure 2.
Corollary
5.5

\ell>2
inert

Figure 3.
Corollary
5.5

\ell>2
ramified, Part I

Figure 4.
Corollary
5.5

\ell>2
ramified, Part II

Corollary 5.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}<-4
, let
\ell>2
, and let
v
be a real vertex in the volcano
\mathcal{G}_{K,\ell,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
.

a)

Suppose
\ell
is unramified in
K
. If
v
is a surface vertex, then there are precisely two real descending edges with initial
vertex
v
. If
v
lies below the surface, there is a unique real descending edge with initial vertex
v
.

b)

Suppose that
\ell
is ramified in
K
. Then there is a unique real descending edge with initial vertex
v
.

Proof.

a) Suppose
v
lies at the surface. If
\ell
is inert in
K
there are no surface edges, so the result follows from Theorem

5.3
. If
\ell
is split there are two surfaces edges, which are interchanged by complex conjugation, so again Theorem
5.3
applies. If
v
lies below the surface, then there is a unique ascending edge with initial vertex
v
,
so it must be stable under complex conjugation. By Theorem
5.3
there is exactly one descending real edge.

b) Suppose
v
lies at the surface. Then there is a unique surface edge with initial vertex
v
, so by Theorem
5.3

there is exactly one descending real edge with initial vertex
v
. If
v
lies below the surface, the argument is the same as that
of part a).
∎

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}<-4
, and suppose that
\ell=2
does not ramify in
K
. Then in the volcano
\mathcal{G}_{K,2,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
:

a)

Every real surface vertex has a unique real descendant.

b)

For
L\in\{1,2\}
, both of the descendants of every real vertex of level
L
are real.

c)

For
L\geq 3
, we partition the real vertices of level
L
into pairs of vertices
\{v_{L},w_{L}\}
, such that
v_{L}
and
w_{L}

are adjacent to the same vertex
u_{L-1}
in level
L-1
. Then exactly one of
v_{L}
and
w_{L}
has two real descendants and the
other has no real descendants.

Figure 5
.
Lemma
5.6

\ell=2
split

Figure 6
.
Lemma
5.6

\ell=2
inert

Proof.

In this case Lemma
2.8
gives

\mathfrak{r}_{1}=\mathfrak{r}_{0},\ \mathfrak{r}_{2}=2\mathfrak{r}_{1},\ \mathfrak{r}_{3}=2\mathfrak{r}_{2},\ \mathfrak{r}_{c+1}=\mathfrak{r}_{c}\ \forall c\geq 3.

a) If
2
is inert in
K
, then every real surface vertex has three descendants. Since
3
is odd, at least one must be real. Since the number of real vertices in level
1
is the same as in level
0
, exactly one must be real, establishing part a) in this case. If
2
splits
in
K
, then every real surface vertex has a unique descending vertex, which must therefore be real.

b) For all
L\geq 1
, every vertex at level
L
has exactly two descendant vertices. Since
\mathfrak{r}_{2}=2\mathfrak{r}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{r}_{3}=2\mathfrak{r}_{2}
, it must be that for
L\in\{1,2\}
every real vertex at level
L
has both of its descendants real.

c) Suppose now that
L\geq 3
and let
v_{L},w_{L}
be a pair of real vertices at level
L
as in the statement of the result. (If
v_{L}

is real and is incident to
u_{L-1}
in level
L-1
, then
u_{L-1}
is real with at least one of its two descendant vertices real, so
the other one,
w_{L}
, must also be real.) If we can show that
v_{L}
and
w_{L}
do not both have descendant real vertices, then
an easy counting argument using
\mathfrak{r}_{L+1}=\mathfrak{r}_{L}
establishes the desired conclusion. So assume now, and let
v_{L+1}
be a real descendant of
v_{L}
and
w_{L+1}
be a real descendant of
w_{L}
. Then

v_{L+1}\mapsto v_{L}\mapsto u_{L-1}\mapsto w_{L}\mapsto w_{L+1}

is a proper, real cyclic
2^{4}
-isogeny with source elliptic curve having discriminant
\Delta=2^{2L+2}\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}
, so
there is a primitive, proper real
\mathcal{O}(\Delta)
-ideal of index
16
. But by Theorem
2.9
, if there is a primitive, proper cyclic real
\mathcal{O}(\Delta)
-ideal of index
2^{a}
, then
a=2
or
a=\operatorname{ord}_{2}(\Delta)-2=2L\geq 6
, a contradiction.
∎

To describe the structure in the next case we need a simple preliminary result.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
\Delta
be an even imaginary quadratic discriminant, let
\mathcal{O}
be the imaginary quadratic order of discriminant
\Delta
, and
let
\mathfrak{p}
be the unique ideal of
\mathcal{O}
of norm
2
. Then
\mathfrak{p}
is principal if and only if
\Delta\in\{-4,-8\}
.

Proof.

For any
N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, there is a principal
\mathcal{O}
-ideal of norm
N
if and only if
N
is integrally represented by the quadratic form
x^{2}+|\frac{\Delta}{4}|y^{2}
. This form represents
2
if and only if
\Delta\in\{-4,-8\}
.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}<-4
, and suppose that
2
ramifies in
K
and that
\operatorname{ord}_{2}(\Delta_{K})=2
. Then in the volcano

\mathcal{G}_{K,2,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
:

a)

The set of real surface vertices is canonically partitioned into pairs
\{v_{0},w_{0}\}
such that
v_{0}
has two real descendants
and
w_{0}
has no real descendants.

b)

Every descendant vertex of a real vertex in level
1
is real.

c)

For
L\geq 2
, we partition the real vertices of level
L
into pairs of vertices
\{v_{L},w_{L}\}
, such that
v_{L}
and
w_{L}

are adjacent to the same vertex
u_{L-1}
in level
L-1
. Then exactly one of
v_{L}
and
w_{L}
has two real descendants and the
other has no real descendants.

Figure 7
.
Lemma
5.8

\ell=2,\ \operatorname{ord}_{2}(\Delta_{K})=2

Proof.

In this case Lemma
2.8
gives

\mathfrak{r}_{1}=\mathfrak{r}_{0},\ \mathfrak{r}_{2}=2\mathfrak{r}_{1},\ \mathfrak{r}_{L+1}=\mathfrak{r}_{L}\ \forall L\geq 2.

a) Let
\mathcal{O}_{0}
be the imaginary quadratic order of discriminant
\mathfrak{f}_{0}^{2}\Delta_{K}
. Since
2
divides
\Delta_{K}
and does not
divide
\mathfrak{f}_{0}
, the ring
\mathcal{O}_{0}
has a unique prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
of norm
2
, which is moreover proper. Since
\mathfrak{p}^{2}=(2)
,
the class
[\mathfrak{p}]\in\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{O}
has order at most
2
, and since
\Delta_{K}=-4
has been excluded, by Lemma
5.7

the class
[\mathfrak{p}]
has order exactly
2
. This implies that there are no surface loops and indeed gives the partition of the
set of surface vertices into pairs: it is the decomposition of
\operatorname{Pic}\mathcal{O}_{0}
into cosets of
\{1,[\mathfrak{p}]\}
. Because
\mathfrak{r}_{1}=\mathfrak{r}_{0}
, a counting argument shows that if the conclusion of part a) did not hold there would be a pair real
vertices
v_{0},w_{0}
linked by a surface edge such that neither
v_{0}
nor
w_{0}
have any real descendants. But in this case
the real elliptic curve corresponding to
v_{0}
would admit no real cyclic
4
-isogeny, whereas as we have observed above,
every real elliptic curve admits a real cyclic
N
-isogeny for all positive integers
N
.

b) This follows from
\mathfrak{r}_{2}=2\mathfrak{r}_{1}
.

c) The argument for this is the same as for Lemma
5.6
c).
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

In the volcano
\mathcal{G}_{\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{-8}),2,1}
:

a)

There is one surface vertex
v_{0}
, which is real.

b)

Both of the descendants
v_{1},w_{1}
of
v_{0}
are real.

c)

For all
L\geq 1
, there are two real vertices
v_{L}
,
w_{L}
in level
L
that are descendants of the same real
vertex
v_{L-1}
in level
L
. The vertex
v_{L}
has two real descendants
v_{L+1}
,
w_{L+1}
, and the vertex
w_{L}

has no real descendants.

Figure 8
.
Lemma
5.9

\ell=2,\Delta_{K}=-8

Proof.

We have
\mathfrak{r}_{1}=2\mathfrak{r}_{0}=2
and
\mathfrak{r}_{L+1}=\mathfrak{r}_{L}=2
for all
L\geq 1
. Also we have

h_{-8}=1
. The claimed structure follows from this, and we leave the details to the reader.
∎

Lemma 5.10
.

Suppose that
\Delta_{K}<-8
and
\operatorname{ord}_{2}(\Delta_{K})=3
. Then in the volcano
\mathcal{G}_{K,2,\mathfrak{f}_{0}}
:

a)

Every descendant vertex of a real surface vertex is real.

b)

For
L\geq 1
, we partition the real vertices of level
L
into pairs of vertices
\{v_{L},w_{L}\}
, such that
v_{L}
and
w_{L}

are adjacent to the same vertex
u_{L-1}
in level
L-1
. Then exactly one of
v_{L}
and
w_{L}
has two real descendants and the
other has no real descendants.

.

Proof.

We have
\mathfrak{r}_{1}=2\mathfrak{r}_{0}
and
\mathfrak{r}_{L+1}=\mathfrak{r}_{L}
for all
