The key takeaway here is that
d_{c}\mid d_{b}
in every case except Case 1.5b).

Theorem 9.2
.

Let
\Delta=\mathfrak{f}^{2}\Delta_{K}
be an imaginary quadratic discriminant with
\Delta_{K}<-4
, and let
M=\ell_{1}^{a_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots\ell_{r}^{a_{r}^{\prime}}\mid N=\ell_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots\ell_{r}^{a_{r}}
. We suppose that
M\in\{1,2\}
. For
1\leq i\leq r
, let

b_{i}\geq 0
be the unique natural number such that
\mathbb{Q}(\ell_{i}^{b_{i}}\mathfrak{f})
occurs up to isomorphism as a primitive residue field of a closed
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(\ell_{i}^{a_{i}^{\prime}},\ell_{i}^{a_{i}})
. Let
c_{i}
be
equal to
b_{i}
if there is a unique primitive residue field of
\Delta
-CM points on
X_{0}(\ell_{i}^{a_{i}^{\prime}},\ell_{i}^{a_{i}})
and otherwise let it be such that the unique non-real primitive residue field of a closed
\Delta
-CM point
on
X_{0}(\ell_{i}^{a_{i}^{\prime}},\ell_{i}^{a_{i}})
is
K(\ell_{i}^{c_{i}}\mathfrak{f})
. Put
B\coloneqq\ell_{1}^{b_{1}}\cdots\ell_{r}^{b_{r}}
and
C\coloneqq\ell_{1}^{c_{1}}\cdots\ell_{r}^{c_{r}}
. Let
s
be the number of
1\leq i\leq r
such that
there is a non-real primitive residue field of a closed
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(\ell_{i}^{a_{i}^{\prime}},\ell_{i}^{a_{i}})
.

a)

If
s=0
, the unique primitive residue field of a
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(M,N)
is
\mathbb{Q}(B\mathfrak{f})
, so the unique primitive degree of a
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(M,N)
is
[\mathbb{Q}(B\mathfrak{f}):\mathbb{Q}]
.

b)

If
s\geq 1
and there is some
1\leq i\leq r
such that there are two primitive residue fields of closed
\Delta
-CM points on
X_{0}(\ell_{i}^{a_{i}^{\prime}},\ell_{i}^{a_{i}})
and we are not in Case 1.5b) with respect to
\Delta
and
\ell_{i}^{a_{i}}
,
then:

(i)

There are two primitive residue fields of
\Delta
-CM points on
X_{0}(M,N)
:
\mathbb{Q}(B\mathfrak{f})
and
K(C\mathfrak{f})
.

(ii)

The unique primitive degree of
\Delta
-CM points on
X_{0}(M,N)
is
[K(C\mathfrak{f}):\mathbb{Q}]
.

c)

If
s\geq 1
and for all
1\leq i\leq r
such that there are two primitive residue fields of closed
\Delta
-CM points on
X_{0}(\ell_{i}^{a_{i}^{\prime}},\ell_{i}^{a_{i}})
we are in Case 1.5b),
then there are two primitive degrees of
\Delta
-CM points on
X_{0}(M,N)
:
[\mathbb{Q}(B\mathfrak{f}):\mathbb{Q}]
and
[K(C\mathfrak{f}):\mathbb{Q}]
.

Proof.

Step 1: The case
s=0
follows from the discussion above. Henceforth we suppose
s\geq 1
. In this case there are (up to isomorphism) two primitive
residue fields of
\Delta
-CM closed points on
X_{0}(M,N)
:
\mathbb{Q}(B\mathfrak{f})
and
K(C\mathfrak{f})
. Put

\mathbf{b}\coloneqq[\mathbb{Q}(B\mathfrak{f}):\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})],\ \mathbf{c}\coloneqq[K(C\mathfrak{f}):\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})].

For each
1\leq i\leq r
, let
F_{i}
be a primitive residue field of a closed point of a
\Delta
-CM elliptic curve on
X_{0}(\ell_{i}^{a_{i}^{\prime}},\ell_{i}^{a_{i}})
; if there is any non-real such field, we take
F_{i}
to be nonreal. Since for each
i
such that there are two primitive residue fields
\mathbb{Q}(\ell_{i}^{b_{i}}\mathfrak{f})
and
K(\ell_{i}^{c_{i}}\mathfrak{f})
we have
[K(\ell_{i}^{c_{i}}\mathfrak{f}):\mathbb{Q}]\leq[\mathbb{Q}(\ell_{i}^{b_{i}}\mathfrak{f}):\mathbb{Q}
], it follows that

\dim_{\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})}F_{1}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})}\otimes\cdots\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})}F_{r}\leq\dim_{\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})}\mathbb{Q}(\ell_{1}^{b_{1}}\mathfrak{f})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})}\cdots\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})}\mathbb{Q}(\ell_{r}^{b_{r}}\mathfrak{f})=[\mathbb{Q}(B\mathfrak{f}):\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})].

Since also

(12)

F_{1}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})}\cdots\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})}F_{r}\cong K(C\mathfrak{f})^{2^{s-1}},

it follows that
\mathbf{c}\leq\mathbf{b}
. Thus there is a unique primitive degree if and only if
\mathbf{c}\mid\mathbf{b}
.

Step 2: Since
K(C\mathfrak{f})\subset K(B\mathfrak{f})=K\mathbb{Q}(B\mathfrak{f})
, we have
\mathbf{c}\mid 2\mathbf{b}
. In particular, we have
\operatorname{ord}_{p}(\mathbf{c})\leq\operatorname{ord}_{p}(\mathbf{b})

for every odd prime
p
. Moreover we have

\operatorname{ord}_{2}(\mathbf{c})=1+\operatorname{ord}_{2}([\mathbb{Q}(C\mathfrak{f}):\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})])=1+\sum_{i=1}^{r}[\mathbb{Q}(\ell^{c_{i}}\mathfrak{f}):\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})]

\operatorname{ord}_{2}(\mathbf{b})=\operatorname{ord}_{2}([\mathbb{Q}(B\mathfrak{f}):\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})])=\sum_{i=1}^{r}[\mathbb{Q}(\ell^{b_{i}}\mathfrak{f}_{:}\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})],

so in the
s\geq 1
case it follows that
\mathbf{c}\mid\mathbf{b}
iff there is some
1\leq i\leq r
such that there are two primitive residue fields of
\Delta
-CM closed points on
X_{0}(\ell_{i}^{a_{i}^{\prime}},\ell_{i}^{a_{i}})

for which we have

\operatorname{ord}_{2}([\mathbb{Q}(\ell^{c_{i}}\mathfrak{f}):\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})])<\operatorname{ord}_{2}([\mathbb{Q}(\ell^{b_{i}}\mathfrak{f}):\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})],

which holds iff

\operatorname{ord}_{2}([K(\ell^{c_{i}}\mathfrak{f}):\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})])\leq\operatorname{ord}_{2}([\mathbb{Q}(\ell^{b_{i}}\mathfrak{f}):\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})].

Consulting the Table above, we see that this holds in every case in which there are two primitive residue fields
except
Case 1.5b).
∎

9.3.
Primitive Residue Fields and Primitive Degrees II

In this section we treat the case in which (
M=2
and
\Delta
is odd) or
M\geq 3
. In this case it follows from §8.3, 8.4 and 9.1 that there is always a unique primitive residue
field, which is a ring class field
K(C\mathfrak{f})
.

Here
C
is computed as follows: let
M=\ell_{1}^{a_{1}^{\prime}}\cdots\ \ell_{r}^{a_{r}^{\prime}}
and
N=\ell_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots\ell_{r}^{a_{r}}
. Fix
1\leq i\leq r
. If the only primitive residue field of a
\Delta
-CM point on

X_{0}(\ell_{i}^{a_{i}^{\prime}},\ell_{i}^{a_{i}})
is
\mathbb{Q}(\ell^{c}\mathfrak{f})
, put
c_{i}\coloneqq c
. If the primitive residue fields of a
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(\ell_{i}^{a_{i}^{\prime}},\ell_{i}^{a_{i}})
are
\mathbb{Q}(\ell^{b}\mathfrak{f})
and
K(\ell^{c}\mathfrak{f})
, put
c_{i}\coloneqq c
. If the only primitive
residue field of a
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(\ell_{i}^{a_{i}^{\prime}},\ell_{i}^{a_{i}})
is
K(\ell^{c}\mathfrak{f})
, put
c_{i}\coloneqq c
. Finally, put

C\coloneqq\ell_{1}^{c_{i}}\cdots\ell_{r}^{c_{r}}.

10.
CM Points of Odd Degree

In this section we will determine all triples
(\Delta,M,N)
for which there is an odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(M,N)
. For each
such triple there is a unique primitive (i.e., not a proper multiple of any other degree of a
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(M,N)
) odd degree and we determine that. From this it follows that for every
(M,N)
such that
X_{0}(M,N)
has odd degree CM points, all such
points have degree multiples of the least such degree
d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{0}(N))
, which we compute. Since all CM elliptic curves over number fields are
\mathbb{Q}
-curves, these results complement recent work of Cremona and Najman, especially
[
CN21
, Thm. 1.1]
.

Using the close relationship between degrees of CM points on
X_{0}(M,N)
and degrees of CM points on
X_{1}(M,N)
given by
Theorem
1.2
for
\Delta<-4
and
[
CGPS
, Thm. 3.7]
for
\Delta\in\{-4,-3\}
, we deduce analogous results for odd degree CM points on
X_{1}(M,N)
. Thus we recover variants of results of Bourdon-Clark
[
BCS17
]
and Bourdon-Pollack
[
BP17
]
.

10.1.
Some Preliminaries

If
\pi:X\rightarrow Y
is a finite
\mathbb{Q}
-morphism of nice curves and
P
is a closed point of
X
, then we have an inclusion of residue fields
\mathbb{Q}(\pi(P))\subset\mathbb{Q}(P)
, and thus the degree of
\pi(P)
divides the degree of
P
. Applying this observation to the morphism
\pi:X_{0}(N)\rightarrow X(1)
we see that in order for a
\Delta
-CM point
P
on
X_{0}(N)
to have odd degree it is necessary that

[\mathbb{Q}(\pi(P)):\mathbb{Q}]=h_{\Delta}
be odd. It follows from Lemma
2.8
that

h_{\Delta}\text{ is odd }\iff\Delta\in\{-4,-8,-12,-16\}\text{ or }\Delta=-2^{\epsilon}\ell^{2L+1},

where
\epsilon\in\{0,2\}
,
\ell\equiv 3\pmod{4}
is a prime number and
L\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.

Lemma 10.1
.

(a)

Let
P
be an odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(4)
. Then
\Delta\in\{-4,-16\}
.

b)

For
\Delta\in\{-4,-16\}
, the unique primitive degree of a
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(4)
is
1
.

Proof.

a) Let
\Delta=\mathfrak{f}^{2}\Delta_{K}
. Because
X_{0}(4)=X_{1}(4)
, it follows from a result of Aoki
[
Ao95
]
(see also
[
BCS17
, Thm. 5.1]
)
that
\Delta_{K}=-4
. Since
h_{\Delta}
must be odd, as above it follows that
\Delta\in\{-4,-16\}
.

b) Consider the elliptic curves

\ (E_{1})_{/\mathbb{Q}}:y^{2}=x^{3}+4x,\ (E_{2})_{/\mathbb{Q}}:y^{2}=x^{3}-11x+14.

Then
E_{1}
has
-4
-CM,
E_{2}
has
-16
-CM and
E_{1}(\mathbb{Q})[\operatorname{tors}]\cong E_{2}(\mathbb{Q})[\operatorname{tors}]\cong\mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

It turns out that there are very few odd degree CM points on
X_{0}(M,N)
with
M\geq 2
.

Proposition 10.2
.

a)

Let
2\leq M\mid N
, and let
P
be an odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(M,N)
. Then
M=N=2
and
\Delta=-4
.

b)

The unique primitive degree of a
-4
-CM point on
X_{0}(2,2)
is
1
.

Proof.

a) Put
\Delta=\mathfrak{f}^{2}\Delta_{K}
. Let
P
be an odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(M,N)
with
M\geq 2
.

\bullet
Suppose first that
M
is divisible by a prime
\ell\geq 3
. Then there is an odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(\ell,\ell)
.
If
\Delta_{K}<-4
this is ruled out by Theorem
6.10
. If
\Delta_{K}=-3
and
\ell\geq 5
we will
show in Proposition
10.8
below that there is not even an odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(1,\ell)
. Finally, if
\Delta_{K}=-3

then we have
X_{0}(3,3)=X_{1}(3,3)
, so the constant subfield of
\mathbb{Q}(X_{0}(3,3))
is
\mathbb{Q}(\zeta_{3})
and every closed point on
X_{0}(3,3)

has even degree.

\bullet
Thus
M
must be a power of
2
, and there is an odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(2,2)=X_{1}(2,2)
. By work of Aoki
[
Ao95
]
and Bourdon-Clark-Stankewicz
[
BCS17
, Thm. 5.1b)]
we must have
\Delta=-4
. This same work
shows that there is no odd degree CM point on
X_{1}(2,4)=X_{0}(2,4)
.

b) The elliptic curve
E_{/\mathbb{Q}}:y^{2}=x^{3}-x
has
-4
-CM and
E(\mathbb{Q})[\operatorname{tors}]\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

In view of Proposition
10.2
we restrict our attention to
X_{0}(N)
.

10.2.
\Delta=-8

Proposition 10.3
.

For
N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, the curve
X_{0}(N)_{/\mathbb{Q}}
has an odd degree
-8
-CM point iff
N\in\{1,2\}
, in which case the unique primitive
degree of
-8
-CM points is
1
.

Proof.

Because
h_{-8}=1
, there is a
\mathbb{Q}
-rational
-8
-CM point on
X(1)
. By §8.2, Case 1a) there is a
\mathbb{Q}
-rational
-8
-CM point on

X_{0}(2)
. By Lemma
10.1
there is no
\mathbb{Q}
-rational
-8
-CM point on
X_{0}(4)
. It remains to rule out the odd degree
-8
-CM
points on
X_{0}(\ell)
for an odd prime
\ell
. It follows from §10.2, Cases 2 and 3 that a primitive residue
field of a
-8
-CM point on
X_{0}(\ell)
is isomorphic to a rational ring class field of discriminant
-8\ell^{2}
or to the ring class field of discriminant
-8
, both of which have even degree.
∎

10.3.
\Delta\in\{-4,-16\}

Proposition 10.4
.

Let
N,\mathfrak{f}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, let
\Delta=-4\mathfrak{f}^{2}
and let
P\in X_{0}(N)
be a
\Delta
-CM point of odd degree. Then:

a)

We have
N\in\{1,2,4\}
and
\Delta\in\{-4,-16\}
.

b)

Conversely, for
N\in\{1,2,4\}
and
\Delta\in\{-4,-16\}
, the unique primitive degree of a
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(N)

is
1
.

Proof.

As above we need
h_{\Delta}
to be odd, and thus
\Delta\in\{-4,-16\}
.

a) We claim that we cannot have
N=\ell
for an odd prime
\ell
. Indeed, let
E
be a
\Delta
-CM elliptic curve
and
\varphi:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
be an
\ell
-isogeny. Then
\varphi
can be proper iff there is an ideal
I
of
\mathcal{O}(\Delta)
of norm
\ell
,
which occurs iff
\ell\equiv 1\pmod{4}
. In this case there are two such ideals that are interchanged by complex conjugation, so
the field of moduli of
\varphi
has even degree. Otherwise
\varphi
is descending, so
\mathbb{Q}(\varphi)
contains the
rational ring class field of discriminant
\ell^{2}\Delta
, which by Proposition
2.1
b) has even degree.
Thus
N
must be a power of
2
.

\bullet
We claim that we cannot have
N=8
.

Let
\varphi:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
be a
cyclic
8
-isogeny of odd degree such that
E
has
-4
-CM and
E^{\prime}
has
(-2^{2L}\cdot 4)
-CM. Because the ring class fields of conductor
-2^{2L}\cdot 4
have even degree for all
L\geq 2
, we must have
L\in\{0,1\}
. If
L=0
then the isogeny is
proper and thus is isomorphic to
E\rightarrow E/E[I]
for a primitive
8
-ideal
I
of
\mathcal{O}(-4)
. Since every ideal of
2
-power order in
the Dedekind domain is a power of the unique prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}_{2}
of norm
2
and
\mathfrak{p}_{2}^{2}=(2)
, there is no primitive
8
-ideal.
If
L=1
then the path in the isogeny graph, since must consist of vertices of level
0
and
1
, must include a downward edge
followed by an upward edge, and by uniqueness of the upward edge from the unique vertex at level
1
, this must be an isogeny
followed by its dual isogeny, hence
\varphi
is not cyclic.

Now let
\varphi:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
be a cyclic
8
-isogeny of odd degree such that
E
has
-16
-CM. As above,
E^{\prime}
must have either
-4
-CM or
-16
-CM. If
E^{\prime}
has
-4
-CM, then
\varphi^{\vee}
is a cyclic
8
-isogeny of the type that was ruled out above.
If
E^{\prime}
has
-16
-CM, then
\varphi
is a proper
8
-isogeny of odd degree, hence of the form
E\rightarrow E/[I]
for a primitive, proper
real ideal
I
. By
[
BCS17
, Thm. 3.7]
there are no such ideals in the quadratic order
\mathcal{O}(-16)
.
Thus
N\mid 4
.

b) This follows from Lemma
10.1
.
∎

10.4.
\Delta=-\ell^{2L+1}
,
3<\ell\equiv 3\pmod{4}
,
L\in\mathbb{N}

Proposition 10.5
.

Let
\ell
be a prime number such that
\ell\equiv 3\pmod{4}
and
\ell>3
. Let
L\in\mathbb{N}
, let
N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, and put

\Delta\coloneqq-\ell^{2L+1}
.

a)

If there is an odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(N)
, then
N\in\{\ell^{a},2\ell^{a}\}
for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.

b)

Let
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. There is an odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(\ell^{a})
, and the unique primitive degree of
such a point is
h_{-\ell}\cdot\ell^{L+\max(a-2L-1,0)}
.

c)

Let
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. There is an odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(2\ell^{a})
, and the unique primitive degree of such a
point is
\begin{cases}3h_{-\ell}\cdot\ell^{L+\max(a-2L-1,0)}&\text{if }\ell\equiv 3\pmod{8}\\
h_{-\ell}\cdot\ell^{L+\max(a-2L-1,0)}&\text{if }\ell\equiv 7\pmod{8}\end{cases}
.

Proof.

a) As in the proof of Proposition
10.3
above, it suffices to show that there is no odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(N)

when
N=4
or
N=p
for a prime
p\nmid 2\ell
. Both are ruled out using Cases 2 and 3 of §9.2, as above.

b) This follows from Case 7 of §9.2 and the identity
h_{-\ell^{2L+1}}=h_{-\ell}\cdot\ell^{L}
(cf. Corollary
2.1
c)).
c) By Case 1 of §9.2, the unique primitive residue field of a
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(2)
is
\mathbb{Q}(2\mathfrak{f})
. It follows that the unique
odd degree primitive residue field of a
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(2\ell^{a})
is the rational ring class field of discriminant

-4\cdot\ell^{2L+2\max(a-2L-1,0)+1}
, which has the degree claimed in the statement.
∎

10.5.
\Delta=-4\cdot\ell^{2L+1}
,
3<\ell\equiv 3\pmod{4}
,
L\in\mathbb{N}

Proposition 10.6
.

Let
3<\ell\equiv 3\pmod{4}
be a prime, let
L,N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, and put
\Delta\coloneqq-4\cdot\ell^{2L+1}
.

a)

If there is an odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(N)
, then
N\in\{\ell^{a},2\ell^{a}\}
for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.

b)

Let
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. There is an odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(\ell^{a})
, and the unique primitive degree of
such a point is
h_{-\ell}\cdot\ell^{L+\max(a-2L-1,0)}
.

c)

Let
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. There is an odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(2\ell^{a})
, and the unique primitive degree of
such a point is
(2-\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right)h_{-\ell}\cdot\ell^{L+\max(a-2L-1,0)}
.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to that of Proposition
10.5
and may be left to the reader.
∎

10.6.
A Useful Lemma

Lemma 10.7
.

Let
\Delta
be an imaginary quadratic discriminant, let
K=\mathbb{Q}(\sqrt{\Delta})
, and let
M\mid N
with
N\geq 3
. Let
d
be the least degree of a
\Delta
-CM point on

X_{0}(M,N)
. Suppose that:

(i)

The least degree
\tilde{d}
of a
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{1}(M,N)_{/\mathbb{Q}}
is equal to the least degree
\tilde{d}_{K}
of a
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{1}(M,N)_{/K}
.

(ii)

We have
\tilde{d}=\frac{\varphi(N)}{2}\cdot d
.

Then
d
is the unique primitive degree of
\Delta
-CM points on
X_{0}(M,N)
.

Proof.

By
[
BC20b
, Thm. 4.1]
, we have that
\tilde{d}
is the unique primitive degree of a
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{1}(M,N)_{/K}
.
Under the natural unramified degree
2
morphism
\nu:X_{1}(M,N)_{/K}\rightarrow X_{1}(M,N)_{/\mathbb{Q}}
if
x\in X_{1}(M,N)_{/K}
is a closed
point, then
\deg(\nu(x))\in\{\deg(x),2\deg(x)\}
, so it follows that
\tilde{d}
is also the unique primitive degree of a
\Delta
-CM
closed point on
X_{0}(M,N)_{/\mathbb{Q}}
. Now consider the natural covering
\pi:X_{1}(M,N)\rightarrow X_{0}(M,N)
, let
P\in X_{0}(M,N)
be a

\Delta
-CM closed point, and let
\tilde{P}\in X_{1}(M,N)
be any closed point such that
\pi(\tilde{P})=P
. Since the corresponding extension of function fields is Galois of degree
\frac{\varphi(N)}{2}
, there is a positive integer
f\mid\frac{\varphi(N)}{2}
such that

\deg(\tilde{P})=f\deg(P).

It follows that

d=\frac{\tilde{d}}{\varphi(N)/2}\mid\frac{\deg(\tilde{P})}{f}=\deg(P).\qed

For all triples
(\Delta,M,N)
the quantity
\tilde{d}_{K}
is computed in
[
BC20b
, Thm. 4.1]
, the quantity
\tilde{d}
is
computed in
[
BC20b
, §8]
. When
M=1
the quantity
d
is computed in
[
CGPS
, Thm. 3.7]
. Thus when
M=1
(the case of
interest to us here) we are always in a position to know whether Lemma
10.7
applies.

Let us give a more explicit desription of the triples
(\Delta,1,N)
for which the conditions (i) and (ii) of Lemma
10.7

applies.

Whether (i) holds depends on the prime power factorization
N=\ell_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots\ell_{r}^{a_{r}}
; the condition (i) holds
for
(\Delta,1,N)
iff it holds for
(\Delta,1,\ell_{i}^{a_{i}})
for all
1\leq i\leq r
. Condition (i) holds for all
(\Delta,1,2)
.
If
\left(\frac{\Delta}{\ell}\right)=1
and
\ell^{a}>2
, then condition (i) does not hold for
(\Delta,1,\ell^{a})
. If
\left(\frac{\Delta}{\ell}\right)=-1
then condition (i) holds for
(\Delta,1,\ell^{a})
. When
\ell^{a}>2
and
\ell\mid\Delta
the conditions are a bit complicated, and we refer to
[
BC20b
, Prop. 6.4 and Thm. 6.5]
for the complete answer but only record the following case for
future use: for all
a,\mathfrak{f}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
, condition (i) holds for
(-3\mathfrak{f}^{2},1,3^{a})
.

By Theorem
1.2
, condition (ii) of Lemma
10.7
always holds when
\Delta<-4
. By Theorem
[
CGPS
, Thm. 3.7]
, for
(-4,1,N)
this condition holds iff
N
is divisible either by
4
or by some prime
\ell\equiv 3\pmod{4}
, while for
(-3,1,N)
this condition holds
iff
N=3
or
N
is divisible either by
9
or by some prime
\ell\equiv 2\pmod{3}
.

10.7.
\Delta=-3^{2L+1}
,
L\in\mathbb{N}

Proposition 10.8
.

Let
\Delta=-3\mathfrak{f}^{2}
for some
\mathfrak{f}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.

a)

Let
N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
be such that there is an odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(N)
. Then

\mathfrak{f}=2^{\epsilon}3^{L}
with
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
and
L\geq 0
. Moreover
N
is
1
,
2
,
3^{a}
for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
or
2\cdot 3^{a}
for some
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.

b)

Suppose
\Delta=-3
.

(i)

For
N=3^{a}
with
a\geq 1
, the unique primitive degree of a
(-3)
-CM point on
X_{0}(N)
is
3^{\max(a-2,0)}
.

(ii)

For
N=2\cdot 3^{a}
with
a\geq 1
, the unique primitive degree of a
(-3)
-CM point on
X_{0}(N)
is
3^{a-1}
.

c)

Suppose
\Delta=-3^{2L+1}
with
L\geq 1
.

(i)

For
N=3^{a}
with
a\geq 1
, the unique primitive degree of a
(-3^{2L+1})
-CM point on
X_{0}(N)

is
3^{L-1+\max(a-2L-1,0)}
.

(ii)

For
N=2\cdot 3^{a}
with
a\geq 1
, the unique primitive degree of a
(-3^{2L+1})
-CM point on
X_{0}(N)

is
3^{L+\max(a-2L-1,0)}
.

d)

Suppose
\Delta=-4\cdot 3^{2L+1}
with
L\geq 0
. For
N\in\{3^{a},2\cdot 3^{a}\}
, the unique primitive degree of a
\Delta
-CM point
on
X_{0}(N)
is
3^{L+\max(a-2L-1,0)}
.

Proof.

a)
\bullet
By Lemma
10.1
, there is no odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(4)
.

\bullet
We claim that for any prime
\ell\nmid 6
, there is no odd degree
\ell
-isogeny
\varphi:E\rightarrow E^{\prime}
with
E
a
\Delta
-CM
elliptic curve. Since
\left(\frac{\Delta}{\ell}\right)\neq 0
, every proper such isogeny would have its field moduli containing
the CM field
K
, hence of even degree. Otherwise the isogeny is decending and the field of moduli would contain the rational
ring class field of discriminant
\ell^{2}\Delta
, which always has even degree.

b), c), d) In all of these cases the conditions of Lemma
10.7
apply and we get that there is a unique primitive degree

d
of a
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(N)
. Moreover
[
CGPS
, Thm. 3.7]
and
[
BC20a
, Thm. 7.2]
compute this degree in
terms of
h_{-\Delta}
, which is in turn easily computed using
h_{-3}=1
and Corollary
2.1
.
∎

10.8.
Compiling Across
\Delta

For a modular curve
X(H)_{/\mathbb{Q}}
we say that
d\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
is a
primitive CM degree

if there is a closed CM point on
X(H)
of degree
d
and there is no closed CM point on
X(H)
of degree properly dividing
d
.

It follows from our previous results that the pairs
(M,N)
for which the modular curve
X_{0}(M,N)
admits an odd degree CM point are:

(1,1)
,
(1,2)
,
(1,4)
,
(1,\ell^{a})
for a prime
\ell\equiv 3\pmod{4}
and
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
,
(1,2\cdot\ell^{a})
for a prime
\ell\equiv 3\pmod{4}
and
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
and
(2,2)
. In this section, for each of these pairs
(M,N)
we will show that there is a unique odd
primitive CM degree
d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{0}(M,N))
, compute
d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{0}(M,N))
, and list all
corresponding
imaginary quadratic discriminants: i.e., those
\Delta
for which there is a
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(M,N)
of degree
d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{0}(M,N))
.

By Proposition 7.2, the only pair
(M,N)
with
M\geq 2
for which there is an odd degree CM point on
X_{0}(M,N)
is
(2,2)
.
This result also shows that
d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{0}(2,2))=1
and the corresponding discriminant is
-4
. Henceforth we take
M=1
.

Proposition 10.9
.

We treat
N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
for which there is a
\mathbb{Q}
-rational CM point on
X_{0}(N)
.

a)

We have
d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{0}(N))=1
iff
N\in\{1,2,3,4,6,7,9,11,14,19,27,43,67,163\}
.

b)

We have
d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{0}(1))=1
. The corresponding discriminants are
-3
,
-4
,
-7
,
-8
,
-11
,
-12
,
-16
,

-19
,
-27
,
-28
,
-43
,
-67
, and
-163
.

c)

We have
d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{0}(2))=1
. The corresponding discriminants are
-3
,
-4
,
-7
,
-8
,
-12
, and
-16
.

d)

We have
d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{0}(3))=1
. The corresponding discriminants are
-3
,
-12
, and
-27
.

e)

We have
d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{0}(4))=1
. The coresponding discriminants are
-4
and
-16
.

f)

We have
d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{0}(6))=1
. The corresponding discriminants are
-3
and
-12
.

g)

For
N\in\{7,14\}
, We have
d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{0}(N))=1
. The corresponding discriminants are
-7
and
-28
.

h)

We have
d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{0}(9))=1
. The corresponding discriminants are
-3
and
-27
.

i)

For
\ell\in\{11,19,43,67\}
, we have
d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{0}(\ell))=1
. The corresponding discriminant is
-\ell
.

j)

We have
d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{0}(27))=1
. The corresponding discriminant is
-27
.

Proof.

We have
d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{0}(N))=1
iff the least degree of a CM point on
X_{0}(N)
is
1
. Since the degree of every

\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(N)
is a multiple of the class number
h_{\Delta}
, we must have
h_{\Delta}=1
, and the list of such discriminants is the one given in part b): this is the solution of the class number one problem due to Heegner, Baker and Stark.
Conversely, for each of the
13
class number one discriminants
\Delta
, it is straightforward to use our previous results on the primitive odd degrees of
\Delta
-CM points on
X_{0}(N)
to find the set of all
N\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
such that there is a
\mathbb{Q}
-rational
\Delta
-CM point on

X_{0}(N)
.
∎

Theorem 10.10
.

Let
\ell\equiv 3\pmod{4}
be a prime number, and let
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
.

a)

Suppose that
\ell>3
and
a
is odd.

(i)

The unique primitive odd degree of a CM point on
X_{0}(\ell^{a})
is

h_{-\ell}\cdot\ell^{\frac{a-1}{2}}
. The corresponding discriminants are
\begin{cases}-\ell^{a}&\text{if }\ell\equiv 3\pmod{8}\\
-\ell^{a},\ -4\cdot\ell^{a}&\text{if }\ell\equiv 7\pmod{8}\end{cases}
.

(ii)

The unique primitive odd degree of a CM point on
X_{0}(2\ell^{a})
is
(2-\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right))h_{-\ell}\cdot\ell^{\frac{a-1}{2}}
. The corresponding discriminants are
-\ell^{a}
and
-4\cdot\ell^{a}
.

b)

Suppose
\ell>3
and
a
is even.

(i)

The unique primitive odd degree of a CM point on
X_{0}(\ell^{a})
is

h_{-\ell}\ell^{a/2}
. The corresponding discriminants are
\begin{cases}-\ell^{a-1},-\ell^{a+1}&\text{if }\ell\equiv 3\pmod{8}\\
-\ell^{a-1},-4\cdot\ell^{a-1},-\ell^{a+1},-4\cdot\ell^{a+1}&\text{if }\ell\equiv 7\pmod{8}\end{cases}
.

(ii)

The unique primitive odd degree of a CM point on
X_{0}(2\cdot\ell^{a})
is
(2-\left(\frac{\ell}{2}\right))h_{-\ell}\ell^{a/2}
.
The corresponding discriminants are
-\ell^{a-1},-4\cdot\ell^{a-1},-\ell^{a+1},-4\cdot\ell^{a+1}
.

c)

Suppose that
\ell=3
and
a\geq 3
is odd.

(i)

The unique primitive odd degree of a CM point on
X_{0}(3^{a})
is
3^{\frac{a-3}{2}}
. The corresponding discriminant is

-3^{a}
.

(ii)

The unique primitive odd degree of a CM point on
X_{0}(2\cdot 3^{a})
is
3^{\frac{a-1}{2}}
. The corresponding discrminants are
-3^{a}
and
-4\cdot 3^{a}
.

d)

Suppose that
\ell=3
and
a\geq 4
is even.

(i)

The unique primitive odd degree of a CM point on
X_{0}(3^{a})
is
3^{\frac{a-2}{2}}
. The corresponding discriminants are

-3^{a-1}
and
-3^{a+1}
.

(ii)

The unique primitive odd degree of a CM point on
X_{0}(2\cdot 3^{a})
is
3^{a/2}
. The corresponding discriminants are

-3^{a-1}
,
-4\cdot 3^{a-1}
,
-3\cdot^{a+1}
and
-4\cdot 3^{a+1}
.

Proof.

a),b) It follows from our previous results that for a prime
\ell
with
3<\ell\equiv\pmod{4}
and
a\geq 1
, if there is an odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(\ell^{a})
, then
\Delta=-\ell^{2L+1}
or
\Delta=-4\ell^{2L+1}
for some
L\geq 0
. Conversely, when this holds, for each
L\geq 0
the unique primitive odd degree
-\ell^{2L+1}
-CM point on
X_{0}(\ell^{a})
is computed by Proposition
10.5
b): it is always
h_{-\ell}
times a power of
\ell
that depends on
a
and
L
. Similarly, Proposition
10.6
computes for each
M\geq 0
the unique primitive odd degree
-4\cdot\ell^{2M+1}
-CM point on
X_{0}(\ell^{a})
: it is alwys
(2-\left(\frac{2}{\ell}\right)h_{-\ell}

times a power of
\ell
that depends on
a
and
M
. Using these formulas it is straightforward to find the values of
L
and/or
M

that minimize the degree and see that this minimal degree divides the degree for each choice of
L
and
M
. The same holds
for
X_{0}(2\ell^{a})
in place of
X_{0}(\ell^{a})
. We leave the details to the reader.

c), d) This proved the same way parts a) and b), using Proposition
10.8
in place of Proposition
10.5
. Again we may leave the details to the reader.
∎

10.9.
Odd Degree CM Points on
X_{1}(M,N)

Proposition 10.11
.

For
\Delta
and
M\mid N
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

There is an odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{1}(M,N)
.

(ii)

There is an odd degree
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(M,N)
.

Proof.

(i)
\implies
(ii) Let
\pi:X_{1}(M,N)\rightarrow X_{0}(M,N)
be the natural map. If
P\in X_{1}(M,N)
is a
\Delta
-CM closed point of odd degree,
then
\pi(P)\in X_{0}(M,N)
is a
\Delta
-CM closed point of odd degree.

(ii)
\implies
(ii) Let
P\in X_{0}(M,N)
be a
\Delta
-CM closed point of odd degree. By our previous results we have

(13)

(M,N)\in\{(1,1),(1,2),(1,4),(1,\ell^{a}),(1,2\ell^{a}),(2,2)\}

for a prime
\ell\equiv 3\pmod{4}
and
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
. For
N\in\{1,2,4\}
the map
\pi
has degree
1
, so the result is
clear. Otherwise
N=2^{\epsilon}\ell^{a}
for some
\epsilon\in\{0,1\}
. In this case we have

\deg(\pi)=\frac{\varphi(2^{\epsilon}\ell^{a})}{2}=\ell^{a-1}\cdot\frac{\ell-1}{2},

which (since
\ell\equiv 3\pmod{4}
) is odd. It follows that some lift of
P
to
\tilde{P}\in X_{1}(M,N)
is an odd degree
\Delta
-CM point.
∎

Corollary 10.12
.

Let
\Delta
and
M\mid N
with
N\geq 3
be such that there is an odd degree
\Delta-
CM closed point on
X_{0}(M,N)
(equivalently, on
X_{1}(M,N)
).

a)

Let
d
be the unique
primitive odd degree of a
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(M,N)
. Then the unique primitive odd degree of a
\Delta
-CM point on

X_{1}(M,N)
is
\frac{\varphi(N)}{2}\cdot d
.

b)

We have

d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{1}(M,N))=\frac{\varphi(N)}{2}\cdot d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{0}(M,N)).

c)

Thus Theorem
10.10
computes
d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{1}(M,N))
in all cases and also determines the corresponding discriminants: they are precisely the same discriminants as for
X_{0}(M,N)
.

Proof.

Part a) holds for
\Delta<-4
by Theorem
1.2
. It holds for
\Delta\in\{-3,-4\}
because the method of
determination of the unique primitive odd degree CM point on
X_{0}(M,N)
in these cases used Lemma
10.7
, which includes
this as a hypothesis. (In all cases in which this result was applied,
[
CGPS
, Cor. 3.7]
was also applied, showing that the minimal degree of a
\Delta
-CM point on
X_{0}(M,N)
is odd.) The remaining parts follow immediately.
∎

10.10.
Comments

The classification of pairs
(M,N)
for which there is an odd degree CM point on
X_{1}(M,N)
attained in Proposition
10.11
is essentially
15
15

15

The latter result is a bit more precise in that it determines which groups
\mathbb{Z}/M\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
arise as the
full
torsion subgroup
E(F)[\operatorname{tors}]
of a CM elliptic curve over some odd degree number field
F
, whereas the
present result determines which groups
\mathbb{Z}/M\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
can be embedded into
E(F)[\operatorname{tors}]
. All discrepancies between the two
results are accounted for by the fact
[
BCS17
, Thm. 4.2]
that if
\Delta<-4
and
\Delta\equiv 1\pmod{8}
then every
\Delta
-CM elliptic curve defined over a number field must have an
F
-rational point of order
2
.
the Odd Degree Theorem of Bourdon-Clark-Stankewicz
[
BCS17
, Thm. 5.3]
. That the set of such pairs
(M,N)
is contained in the list of (
13
) had been obtained earlier by Aoki
[
Ao95
]
.

The computation of
d_{\operatorname{odd-CM}}(X_{1}(M,N))
for all the
(M,N)
listed in (
13
) is essentially
16
16

16

Same proviso as above.

the Strong Odd Degree Theorem of Bourdon-Pollack
[
BP17
, Thm. 2.12]
. Our computation of the corresponding discriminants in each case – i.e., the complete list of
\Delta
for which the minimum odd degree CM point on
X_{1}(M,N)
is a
\Delta
-CM point –
is a further refinement, a “Stronger Odd Degree Theorem.” However there is also
[
BP17
, Remark 2.7]
, which gives
most (but not all) of the restrictions on the corresponding discriminants in the case
N=\ell^{a}
. That this is a Remark rather than a result seems to indicate that Bourdon and Pollack were not as interested in this refinement; it may well be that
they could have proven the Stronger Odd Degree Theorem for
X_{1}(M,N)
using their methods.

The relationship between odd degree CM points on
X_{0}(M,N)
versus
X_{1}(M,N)
is interesting. The implication (i)
\implies
(ii)
in Proposition
10.11
is completely routine: of course if there is an odd degree CM point on
X_{1}(M,N)
then its image on
X_{0}(M,N)

is also an odd degree CM point. But the converse implication (ii)
\implies
(i) seems a bit surprising, even in retrospect. One way to view this is: the fact that every
N\geq 3
that appears in (
13
) has
\frac{\varphi(N)}{2}
odd seemed to play an important
role in the Odd Degree Theorem on
X_{1}(M,N)
: indeed the proof of the Odd Degree Theorem used the fact that if
P\in X_{1}(N)

is a CM point of odd degree
d
, then
\frac{\varphi(N)}{2}h_{\Delta}\mid d

[
BCS17
, Thm. 4.12d)]
. One might have
expected there to be odd degree CM points on
X_{0}(N)
for values of
N\geq 3
for which
\frac{\varphi(N)}{2}
is even, which
therefore would not give rise to odd degree CM points on
X_{1}(N)
. This turns out not to be the case, but our argument does not directly explain why.

Although the structure of odd degree CM points on
X_{0}(M,N)
is exactly the same as that of odd degree CM points on
X_{1}(M,N)
,
nevertheless we can deduce the results on
X_{1}(M,N)
from those on
X_{0}(M,N)
, but not conversely, which seems to indicate once again that the curves
X_{0}(M,N)
are fundamental. There is however an exception to this: whereas for
\Delta_{K}<-4
our discussion on

X_{0}(M,N)
uses the description of primitive degrees of CM points that lies at the heart of the present work, for
\Delta_{K}\in\{-4,-3\}
we use some observations about isogeny graphs but also Lemma
10.7
. Our application of Lemma
10.7
in turn uses results of degrees of CM points on
X_{1}(M,N)_{/\mathbb{Q}}
and
X_{1}(M,N)_{/K}
due to Bourdon and Clark
[
BC20a
]
,
[
BC20b
]
. Thus from
the perspective of treating
X_{0}(M,N)
as fundamental it is “a cheat.” This again points to the need to complete the present
results with a treatment of
\Delta_{K}\in\{-4,-3\}
, but we did not want to allow this incompleteness to yield incomplete information
in the odd degree case.

Primitive Residue Fields:

\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})

Again
b=0
yields the closed point equivalence class

[P_{\downarrow}]
with residue field
\mathbb{Q}(2^{a}\mathfrak{f})
, while
b=a
yields the path
P_{\uparrow}
, with residue field
\mathbb{Q}(\mathfrak{f})
. For all paths with
b=1
, the initial subpath
P_{1}
consists of one ascent followed by one (unique) descent, so
P_{1}
is real, and we get one closed point equivalence class of paths with residue field
\mathbb{Q}(2^{a-2}\mathfrak{f})
. It follows from Lemmas
5.6
,
5.8
,

5.9
and
5.10
that for
2\leq b\leq a-1
the segment
P_{1}
is not real and thus
the residue field contains
K
. For
2\leq b<A
the path
P_{1}
consists of
b
ascents followed by
b
descents. There are

2^{b-1}
such paths, which fall into
2^{b-2}
closed point equivalence classes. There are
a-2b
further descents, so all in
all there are
2^{b-1}
closed point equivalence classes of paths, each with residue field
K(2^{a-2b}\mathfrak{f})
.

b=1

\mathbb{Q}(2^{a-2}\mathfrak{f})
. For
2\leq b\leq A
, by the same
results cited above the second descent means that the residue field contains
K
, and there are
2^{b-2}
closed point equivalence
classes of such paths, each with residue field

. For

K
and
there are
2^{a-b-2}

K(\mathfrak{f})
.
